🔄 The 4 GitOps Principles — The Foundation of Everything

Every question in this guide traces back to one of these four principles. Master them, and you master GitOps.

📋
1. Declarative
Desired state described declaratively — WHAT you want, not HOW to achieve it. YAML, not scripts.
Q41–Q52
🔒
2. Versioned & Immutable
Every state change = immutable Git commit. SHA digests, not mutable tags. Complete audit trail.
Q53–Q64
⬇️
3. Pulled Automatically
Reconciler pulls from Git — no CI pushes to cluster. Credentials stay inside. Self-healing enabled.
Q65–Q76
🔄
4. Continuously Reconciled
Reconciler runs forever — not one-shot. Detects drift, auto-corrects. The cluster fights back.
Q77–Q100
🔄 The GitOps Reconciliation Loop — How Every Deployment Works
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        THE GITOPS RECONCILIATION LOOP                        │
  │                                                                              │
  │   ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐       │
  │   │ ① DEVELOPER│      │ ② GIT    │      │ ③ RECON- │      │ ④ KUBER- │       │
  │   │  git push  │─────▶│  REPO    │─────▶│  CILER   │─────▶│  NETES   │       │
  │   │  changes   │      │ (Truth)  │      │ (ArgoCD/ │      │ CLUSTER  │       │
  │   └──────────┘      └──────────┘      │  Flux)   │      └──────────┘       │
  │                                       │  polls   │           │              │
  │                                       │  diffs   │           │              │
  │                                       │  applies │           │              │
  │                                       └──────────┘           │              │
  │                                            │                 │              │
  │                                            │  ⑤ FEEDBACK     │              │
  │                                            │  Sync status ◄──┘              │
  │                                            │  Health status                 │
  │                                            │  Drift detected                │
  │                                            ▼                                │
  │                                       ┌──────────┐                          │
  │                                       │ ⑥ ARGOCD │  ← UI / CLI / Notify    │
  │                                       │ DASHBOARD│     Synced + Healthy?    │
  │                                       └──────────┘                          │
  │                                                                              │
  │  KEY PROPERTIES:                                                             │
  │  • Pull-based: Reconciler pulls from Git (cluster creds never leave)         │
  │  • Self-healing: Drift auto-corrected within 3 minutes                       │
  │  • Git as SSOT: Git IS the single source of truth for your entire platform   │
  │  • Immutable audit: Every change = Git commit with SHA, author, timestamp    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Lock Down
Branch protection ON
PR reviews required
No force-push
~2 days

ArgoCD auto-syncs
Separate CI & CD credentials
~1 week

Prometheus metric gates
Auto-rollback on failure
~2 weeks

Multi-Cluster
Hub-spoke ArgoCD
ApplicationSet generator
Air-gapped DR ready
~2 weeks

For anihpj/jobpost: Phases 1-3 are already done (Git repo, ArgoCD installed, branch protection on). Phase 4 (CI→CD automation) is in progress. Phase 5 (progressive delivery) is the 3-month target. Phase 6 when expanding beyond 6 clusters.

✅ What GitOps Gives You — Before vs After

❌ WITHOUT GitOps
  • • CI needs cluster credentials (security risk)
  • • "Who changed what?" — no audit trail
  • • Rollback = manual kubectl (error-prone)
  • • Drift goes undetected for days/weeks
  • • Deploy scripts scattered across Jenkins
  • • No self-healing — manual recovery only
✅ WITH GitOps (This Guide)
  • • Cluster creds never leave the cluster
  • • Every change = Git commit + SHA + author
  • • Rollback = git revert + push (30 seconds)
  • • Drift detected & auto-corrected in 3 minutes
  • • All config in one Git repo — single source of truth
  • • Self-healing: cluster fights back against drift

⚔️ ArgoCD vs Flux — Choosing Your Reconciler

🦑 ArgoCD
ArchitectureMonolithic (API + Repo + Controller)
UI✅ Rich Web Dashboard
Multi-Cluster✅ Hub-Spoke (central control)
SSO✅ GitHub/GitLab/OIDC
Sync MechanismPoll (3 min) + Webhooks
Image Updater⚠️ Separate (argocd-image-updater)
Best ForTeams wanting UI, SSO, central mgmt
⚡ Flux CD
ArchitectureModular (Separate Controllers)
UI⚠️ CLI + optional Web UI
Multi-Cluster✅ Per-cluster independence
SSO⚠️ Via Git provider + RBAC
Sync MechanismPoll + Webhooks (Notification Ctrl)
Image Updater✅ Built-in (Image Automation)
Best ForGitOps-native, modular, air-gapped

💡 anihpj strategy: ArgoCD on kubeadm/kind (prod/staging/dev) for UI + SSO. Flux on kind-cl3 (DR) for S3 bucket source + image automation. Both coexist — same Git repo, different reconcilers.

📖 GitOps Lexicon — The 9 Terms You MUST Know

📋 Desired State
WHAT should be running. Declared in Git as YAML. The single source of truth.
🔄 State Drift
When cluster ≠ Git. Caused by manual kubectl, failed syncs, or external changes.
♻️ Reconciliation
Continuous process: compare Git vs cluster → fix differences → repeat forever.
🗄️ State Store
Where desired state lives — Git repo, S3 bucket, OCI registry. Git is the default.
🔁 Feedback Loop
Reconciler reports sync/health status back. ArgoCD dashboard = visual feedback.
⏪ Rollback
git revert + git push. No special procedure. Same mechanism as deploy — just reverse.
🔒 Immutability
History cannot be changed. SHA digests, not tags. Every deployment = permanent record.
🤖 Self-Healing
Reconciler auto-corrects drift. Delete a pod? ArgoCD recreates it. Cluster fights back.
⬇️ Pull Model
Reconciler pulls from Git — no CI pushes to cluster. Credentials stay inside the cluster.

⚠️ Common GitOps Anti-Patterns — What NOT To Do

❌ CI Pushes to Cluster
CI runs kubectl apply. Breaks pull model. Exposes cluster credentials. No self-healing. Fix: CI writes to Git, reconciler pulls.
❌ kubectl During Incidents
"Emergency" kubectl edit bypasses Git. Creates drift. ArgoCD reverts your fix. Fix: git revert + push, even at 3 AM.
❌ Secrets in Plain Git
Never commit raw secrets. Use SealedSecrets, SOPS, or External Secrets Operator. Git is NOT a vault.
⚠️ Auto-Sync OFF in Prod
Disabling auto-sync removes self-healing. Use sync windows instead for blackout periods. Re-enable after.
⚠️ Storing YAML Without Reconciler
"I store YAML in Git" ≠ GitOps. You need a reconciler continuously syncing. Otherwise it's just Git-backed CI/CD.
⚠️ Branch-Based Deploy Targets
ArgoCD tracking "main" branch is fine. But "deploy prod from prod-branch" creates drift between branches. Use directory-based environments instead.
⏱️ anihpj/jobpost Deployment Timeline — From git push to Production
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    ANIHPJ/JOBPOST DEPLOYMENT — END TO END                    │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

✅ GitOps Implementation Checklist — Are You Ready?

☐All K8s manifests stored in Git
☐Reconciler installed (ArgoCD/Flux)
☐Reconciler uses read-only Git access
☐Branch protection enabled (no force-push)
☐PR review required before merge
☐CI writes to Git — NOT kubectl apply
☐Auto-sync enabled (or sync windows configured)
☐Secrets managed via SealedSecrets/SOPS/ESO
☐Image references use SHA256 digests
☐Drift alerts configured (Slack/PagerDuty)
☐Multi-environment structure (base + overlays)
☐Team trained: "Change Git, not cluster"

✅ All 12 checked? You're doing GitOps. Any unchecked? That's your next implementation target. Print and post on the team wall.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "Why is ArgoCD showing OutOfSync?" — No idea how to check drift
  • ❌ "I committed YAML but nothing deployed" — Don't know about sync intervals
  • ❌ "I updated the image tag but the old version is running" — Didn't know about pull reconciliation
  • ❌ "Someone kubectl-edited my deployment and it broke" — Never set up self-healing
  • ❌ "My canary deploy is sending 100% traffic on day one" — No progressive delivery
  • ❌ "I ran kubectl commands on the wrong cluster" — No context discipline
  • ❌ "Rollback? I just kubectl apply the old YAML... wait, which was the old one?" — No Git versioning

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Diagnose any GitOps sync failure in <2 min using the reconciliation loop
  • ✅ Set up ArgoCD/Flux for automated pull-based deployment from Git
  • ✅ Implement progressive delivery: canary, blue-green with Argo Rollouts
  • ✅ Manage multi-environment GitOps with Kustomize overlays and ApplicationSets
  • ✅ Detect and auto-correct configuration drift via continuous reconciliation
  • ✅ Roll back ANY change by reverting a Git commit — full audit trail
  • ✅ Integrate GitOps with CI, observability, and notification systems

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Eliminate obviously wrong answers first — each elimination increases your odds from 25% to 33% to 50%.
  • ✅ DO: Watch for ABSOLUTE words: "always", "never", "must", "only" — these are often exam traps in multiple-choice questions.
  • ✅ DO: Prioritize Principles (30%) and Terminology (20%) questions — together they're 50% of your score.
  • ✅ DO: Read ALL answer options before selecting — the first plausible answer isn't always the BEST answer.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 90 seconds on any single question. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Second-guess yourself excessively. Your first instinct is usually correct if you've studied.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any question blank — there's no penalty for wrong answers. Always make your best guess.
  • ❌ DON'T: Panic if you see unfamiliar tool names. Focus on the GitOps PRINCIPLES — they apply regardless of the tool.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

GitOps Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 90 minutes | Format: Multiple-choice (online proctored) | Passing Score: 75% | Cost: $250 (includes one free retake) | Certification Validity: 2 years

5 Official Domains: GitOps Terminology (20%), GitOps Principles (30%), Related Practices (16%), GitOps Patterns (20%), Tooling (14%). Principles is the heaviest — focus your study time accordingly.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Answer easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → Eliminate wrong answers systematically. The exam tests your understanding of GitOps METHODOLOGY — not memorization of specific tool commands.

Your 6-cluster lab (below) mirrors real GitOps environments. Use ksw to switch between clusters for hands-on GitOps practice.

📚 Pre-Learning: GitOps FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your GitOps Mental Model

F.1 What Is GitOps & Why Does It Matter? — The evolution from manual kubectl → CI/CD pipelines → GitOps. The 4 principles that define GitOps. Why "Git as the single source of truth" is a paradigm shift.

F.2 GitOps Terminology — The Lexicon You MUST Know — Desired State, State Drift, State Reconciliation, State Store, Feedback Loop, Rollback, Continuous Reconciliation. Every term defined with analogies.

F.3 The GitOps Reconciliation Loop — How It Actually Works — Git Repo → Reconciler polls/clones → Compares with cluster state → Creates/Updates/Deletes → Reports status back. Polling vs Webhooks. Self-healing property.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application Under GitOps — Full GitOps architecture: environments/ (dev/staging/prod), Kustomize overlays, ArgoCD Applications, CI → Git → Reconciler → Cluster flow.

F.5 GitOps In Your 6-Cluster Lab — Hands-on environment: kubeadm (ArgoCD installed), kind clusters (multi-cluster patterns), minikube (quick tests). Using ksw to switch between clusters safely.

If you're new to GitOps: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the MCQs. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example.

📝 Part 1: 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Complete GitOps MCQ Bank — Organized by the 5 official CNCF exam domains. Each question includes a detailed explanation, diagrams, and real-world context using anihpj/jobpost.

200Questions
5Categories

📝 Category 1: GitOps Terminology (20%)Q1-Q40

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Understanding GitOps Vocabulary

This category is about the language of GitOps — the 9 core terms that define the methodology. Think of GitOps terminology like learning the grammar of a new language: Desired State (what you want), State Drift (deviation from what you want), Reconciliation (continuously fixing drift), and State Store (where the truth lives — Git). For anihpj/jobpost, understanding these terms means knowing that the Git repo IS the control plane for your entire deployment.

Q1-Q40 40 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 Continuous & Declarative DescriptionQ1-Q6

GitOps Terminology Q1 of 200
What does "Continuous" mean in the context of GitOps?
A) Continuous means the reconciliation process is ONGOING — not a one-time deployment. The reconciler keeps watching the state store and the cluster, constantly comparing and correcting. If someone manually deletes a Deployment, the reconciler recreates it
B) Continuous means the CI pipeline runs on every git push, building and testing code automatically
C) Continuous means the application runs 24/7 without any downtime or maintenance windows
D) Continuous means the same deployment is repeated across multiple clusters in a sequence
✅ Correct Answer: A

"Continuous" = not "fire and forget." Traditional CI/CD pushes code once and stops. GitOps pulls continuously — the reconciler keeps watching, comparing, and correcting forever. It's an infinite loop, not a one-shot operation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Traditional Push (One-Time) vs GitOps Pull (Continuous)
  TRADITIONAL CI/CD (Push — One-Time)      GITOPS (Pull — Continuous)
  ┌─────────────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① git push → CI builds     │         │ ① git push → Git updated     │
  │ ② CI runs kubectl apply    │         │ ② Reconciler polls Git       │
  │ ③ CI exits — DONE          │         │ ③ Diff: Git vs Cluster       │
  │                             │         │ ④ Apply changes if needed    │
  │ ❌ No self-healing          │         │ ⑤ Report status → LOOP 🔄    │
  │ ❌ CI needs cluster creds   │         │ ⑥ Poll again in 3 min...     │
  │ ❌ "Who changed what?"      │         │                              │
  └─────────────────────────────┘         │ ✅ Self-healing              │
                                           │ ✅ Cluster creds stay inside │
                                           │ ✅ Every change = Git commit │
                                           └─────────────────────────────┘

  Analogy: A thermostat doesn't check the temperature ONCE and say "done."
  It continuously monitors and adjusts — heating when cold, stopping when
  warm. GitOps is the thermostat for your cluster's configuration.
🔬 ArgoCD Polling — The Implementation of "Continuous"
ArgoCD Reconciliation Cycle — The 3-Minute Loop
  TIME  ACTION                                    SYNC STATUS
  ────  ────────────────────────────────────────  ───────────
  T0    git push (new commit a1b2c3d)             OutOfSync
  T1    ArgoCD polls Git (every 3 min)            OutOfSync
  T2    git fetch → detects new commit a1b2c3d    OutOfSync
  T3    Render manifests (Kustomize/Helm)          OutOfSync
  T4    3-way diff: Git vs Live vs Last-Synced     OutOfSync
  T5    Apply changes (kubectl apply equivalent)   Progressing
  T6    Wait for health checks (readiness probes)  Progressing
  T7    All pods Ready, traffic flowing            Synced ✅
  ────  ────────────────────────────────────────  ───────────
  T8    Someone runs: kubectl delete deploy anihpj-api
  T9    ArgoCD polls again → Deployment missing!   OutOfSync
  T10   ArgoCD recreates Deployment from Git       Progressing
  T11   Pods Ready → job board back online         Synced ✅
  ────  ────────────────────────────────────────  ───────────

  Total downtime from accidental delete: ~4 minutes (3 min poll + ~1 min deploy)
  Without GitOps: app stays DOWN until a human notices and manually fixes it.

This loop never stops. Even if nothing changes in Git, ArgoCD keeps checking — because "nothing changed" is STILL a valid observation. This is what makes GitOps self-healing: if someone manually edits a resource in the cluster, the next reconciliation cycle detects the drift and corrects it back to the Git-defined state.

For anihpj/jobpost: ArgoCD continuously reconciles the anihpj namespace on the kubeadm cluster. If someone accidentally runs kubectl delete deploy anihpj-api -n anihpj, ArgoCD detects the missing Deployment within 3 minutes and recreates it from Git — the job board comes back automatically. No human intervention needed.

GitOps Terminology Q2 of 200
What is a "Declarative Description" in GitOps?
A) A statement of WHAT you want (the desired end state), not HOW to achieve it. "I want 3 replicas of anihpj-api running" is declarative. "Run kubectl scale --replicas=3" is imperative
B) A detailed step-by-step script that the CI pipeline executes to deploy the application
C) A natural language description written in the README.md explaining how to deploy the application
D) A Dockerfile that describes how the container image should be built from source code
✅ Correct Answer: A

Declarative = describe the destination, not the journey. Kubernetes itself is declarative — you write YAML stating your desired state (3 replicas, this image, that port). GitOps extends this to the entire delivery pipeline — Git IS your declarative description. The reconciler figures out HOW to make the cluster match.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Imperative (Step-by-Step Script) vs Declarative (Desired State YAML)
  IMPERATIVE APPROACH (Script)              DECLARATIVE APPROACH (YAML in Git)
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ #!/bin/bash                  │         │ apiVersion: apps/v1          │
  │ ssh prod-server              │         │ kind: Deployment             │
  │ cd /opt/anihpj               │         │ metadata:                    │
  │ git pull origin main         │         │   name: anihpj-api           │
  │ pip install -r requirements  │         │ spec:                        │
  │ python manage.py migrate     │         │   replicas: 3                │
  │ sudo systemctl restart gunic │         │   selector:                  │
  │ # Check if it worked?        │         │     matchLabels:             │
  │ curl localhost:8000/health   │         │       app: anihpj-api         │
  │                               │         │   template:                  │
  │ ❌ Each step = potential fail │         │     spec:                    │
  │ ❌ No version history of steps│         │       containers:            │
  │ ❌ "Did Bob run this or not?" │         │       - image: anihpj-api:v2 │
  │ ❌ Can't diff before/after    │         │         ports: [8000]        │
  └──────────────────────────────┘         │                              │
                                           │ ✅ Single source of truth     │
                                           │ ✅ Git versioned every change │
                                           │ ✅ Reconciler handles the HOW │
                                           │ ✅ Diffable — see what changed│
                                           └──────────────────────────────┘

  Key insight: The declarative YAML doesn't say "run kubectl apply."
  It just declares: "3 replicas of anihpj-api:v2 should exist on port 8000."
  The reconciler (ArgoCD/Flux) reads this and makes it happen.
🔬 The Reconciler's Job — Turning "What" into "How"
6-Step Reconciliation Flow — From Git to Cluster
  GIT REPO                          RECONCILER                         CLUSTER
  ┌──────────┐         ┌──────────────────────────────┐         ┌──────────────┐
  │ anihpj-  │──pull──▶│ ① READ GIT                   │         │              │
  │ gitops/  │         │ Clone/fetch manifest repo    │         │  kubeadm     │
  │ environ/ │         │                              │         │  cluster     │
  │  prod/   │         │ ② RENDER                     │         │              │
  │  *.yaml  │         │ Kustomize overlay / Helm tmpl│         │ ┌──────────┐ │
  └──────────┘         │ → Final Kubernetes objects   │         │ │anihpj ns │ │
                        │                              │         │ │          │ │
                        │ ③ DIFF                       │──GET──▶│ │┌────────┐│ │
                        │ Compare: desired(Git) vs     │◀─LIVE──│ ││anihpj- ││ │
                        │ actual(Cluster) vs last-sync │         │ ││api pod ││ │
                        │                              │         │ │└────────┘│ │
                        │ ④ PLAN                       │         │ └──────────┘ │
                        │ Determine: CREATE/UPDATE/    │         │              │
                        │ DELETE which resources?      │         └──────────────┘
                        │                              │
                        │ ⑤ EXECUTE                    │──APPLY─▶  CLUSTER
                        │ kubectl apply (or API call)  │          UPDATED
                        │                              │
                        │ ⑥ VERIFY                     │──CHECK─▶  HEALTH
                        │ Readiness probes + status    │◀STATUS──  PROBES
                        │ Report: Synced + Healthy ✅   │
                        │                              │
                        │ ⑦ LOOP 🔄 (back to step ①)  │
                        └──────────────────────────────┘

  YOU declare WHAT (YAML in Git) → Reconciler figures out HOW → Cluster matches.

This is the essence of GitOps: YOU declare WHAT. Git stores it. The reconciler does HOW. For anihpj/jobpost, the entire application — Django API, PostgreSQL, Redis, Ingress, NetworkPolicy, HPA, RBAC — is declared across ~15 YAML files in the environments/ directory. ArgoCD reads them all and ensures the cluster matches.

GitOps Terminology Q3 of 200
How does "Declarative" differ from "Imperative" in an anihpj/jobpost deployment?
A) Imperative: You SSH to the server, run "git pull", "pip install", "python manage.py migrate", "gunicorn..." — each step manually. Declarative: You write a Deployment YAML saying "image: anihpj-api:v1.2.4, replicas: 3, port: 8000" — and ArgoCD makes it happen. No manual steps, no tribal knowledge
B) Imperative uses kubectl commands; Declarative uses the Kubernetes dashboard UI
C) There is no practical difference — both achieve the same result with different syntax
D) Imperative is for development environments; Declarative is only used in production
✅ Correct Answer: A

The declarative approach means your ENTIRE deployment process is encoded in Git — not in someone's head, not in a wiki, not in "Bob's runbook." No tribal knowledge. No "Bob ran a script last Tuesday." The desired state declares EVERYTHING, and the reconciler ensures it stays that way.

📝 Detailed Explanation — anihpj/jobpost Deployment: Two Worlds
Imperative Workflow (Pre-GitOps) vs Declarative Workflow (GitOps)
  IMPERATIVE — "Bob Deploys anihpj"           DECLARATIVE — "GitOps Deploys anihpj"
  ┌────────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 😰 Bob's Monday Morning:       │         │ ✅ GitOps Continuous Flow:      │
  │                                │         │                                │
  │ 1. SSH to prod-server          │         │ 1. Developer edits Deployment  │
  │ 2. cd /opt/anihpj              │         │    YAML in Git (replicas: 3→5) │
  │ 3. git pull (hope no conflicts)│         │ 2. git commit + git push       │
  │ 4. source venv/bin/activate    │         │ 3. ArgoCD detects change (poll │
  │ 5. pip install -r requirements │         │    or webhook, within 3 min)   │
  │ 6. python manage.py migrate    │         │ 4. ArgoCD diffs: Git vs Cluster│
  │ 7. python manage.py collectsta │         │ 5. ArgoCD applies: scale to 5  │
  │ 8. sudo systemctl restart gunic│         │ 6. Health check: pods Ready ✅  │
  │ 9. Check if site is up...      │         │ 7. Status: Synced + Healthy ✅  │
  │ 10. "Works on my machine!"     │         │                                │
  │                                │         │ 📦 Git commit = immutable record│
  │ ❌ Bob is now a SPOF           │         │ 👀 PR review = peer validation  │
  │ ❌ No record of exact steps    │         │ 🔄 Self-healing = auto-correct  │
  │ ❌ Bob on vacation = no deploy │         │ 🏖️ Bob can be on vacation!     │
  └────────────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────────────────┘
🔬 The "Tribal Knowledge" Problem — Solved by Declarative GitOps
Knowledge Transfer: Imperative World vs GitOps World
  IMPERATIVE WORLD (Knowledge in People)    GITOPS WORLD (Knowledge in Git)
  ┌────────────────────────────────┐       ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 👨‍💻 Bob: 10-step deploy script   │       │ 📁 environments/prod/*.yaml     │
  │ 👩‍💻 Alice: 8-step deploy script  │       │                                │
  │ 🧑‍💻 Carol: "I think Bob showed me" │       │ ✅ Single source of truth       │
  │                                │       │ ✅ Anyone can read the YAML     │
  │ Bob leaves → Knowledge GONE    │       │ ✅ Git blame = who changed what │
  │ Alice on vacation → No deploy  │       │ ✅ PR review = peer validation   │
  │ "Dave ran something... what?"  │       │ ✅ ArgoCD sync = no human needed│
  │                                │       │                                │
  │ Risk: BUS factor = 1 (Bob)     │       │ Risk: BUS factor = 0 (Git)      │
  │ Recovery: Ask Bob (impossible) │       │ Recovery: git clone + sync      │
  └────────────────────────────────┘       └────────────────────────────────┘

  BUS Factor = "Bus Factor" — how many people must be unavailable before
  the project is in trouble. Imperative world: BUS factor = 1 (Bob).
  GitOps world: BUS factor = 0 — Git is always available.

In the imperative world, deployment knowledge lives in people's heads. In the declarative GitOps world, deployment knowledge lives in Git. The YAML files ARE the deployment procedure. ArgoCD ensures it IS running. No Bob required.

For anihpj/jobpost: the environments/prod/ directory in Git contains every YAML file that defines the production state. If the entire cluster burned down, you could rebuild it from those Git files alone — no tribal knowledge, no runbooks, no Bob. Just git clone + ArgoCD sync = complete recovery.

GitOps Terminology Q4 of 200
Why is "Declarative" the FIRST of the 4 GitOps Principles?
A) Because the entire system MUST be described declaratively. Without declarative configuration, you can't version it, you can't reconcile it, you can't detect drift. Declarative is the FOUNDATION — the other 3 principles (Versioned, Pulled, Continuously Reconciled) depend on it
B) Because it's alphabetically first among the 4 principle names
C) Because Kubernetes was the first tool to use declarative configuration, and GitOps inherited it
D) Because the CNCF GitOps Working Group voted to list it first for historical reasons
✅ Correct Answer: A

Declarative is the prerequisite for the other 3 principles. If your config is imperative ("run this script"), the reconciler can't compare desired vs actual — there's no static "desired state" to compare against. Declarative configs are the foundation upon which versioning, pulling, and continuous reconciliation are built.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The "House of GitOps"
The 4 GitOps Principles — Dependency Hierarchy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    THE GITOPS HOUSE                              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  ④ CONTINUOUSLY RECONCILED                               │  │
  │  │  "The reconciler keeps watching forever — self-healing"   │  │
  │  │  Depends on: ③ (must be pulling to reconcile)            │  │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │  ③ PULLED AUTOMATICALLY                                  │  │
  │  │  "Reconciler pulls from Git — no CI pushes to cluster"    │  │
  │  │  Depends on: ② (must be versioned to pull specific state) │  │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │  ② VERSIONED & IMMUTABLE                                  │  │
  │  │  "Every state change = Git commit with SHA — auditable"   │  │
  │  │  Depends on: ① (must be declarative to version diffs)     │  │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │  ① DECLARATIVE  ← THE FOUNDATION                         │  │
  │  │  "Entire system described declaratively — WHAT not HOW"   │  │
  │  │  If this cracks, the whole house collapses.               │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                 │
  │  WITHOUT ①: You can't version imperative scripts meaningfully.  │
  │             You can't pull "desired state" that doesn't exist.  │
  │             You can't reconcile against an unknown target.      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔬 Concrete Example — Why Declarative Must Come First
The deploy.sh Problem — Why Imperative Config Breaks GitOps
  DEPLOY.SH (Imperative)                    DEPLOYMENT.YAML (Declarative)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ #!/bin/bash                │           │ apiVersion: apps/v1        │
  │ if [ "$ENV" = "prod" ]; then│           │ kind: Deployment           │
  │   REPLICAS=5               │           │ spec:                      │
  │ else                       │           │   replicas: 3              │
  │   REPLICAS=2               │           │   template:                │
  │ fi                         │           │     spec:                  │
  │ kubectl scale deploy/api   │           │       containers:          │
  │   --replicas=$REPLICAS     │           │       - image: api:v1.2.4  │
  └────────────────────────────┘           └────────────────────────────┘

  CAN THE RECONCILER USE THIS?              CAN THE RECONCILER USE THIS?
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ ❌ Must EXECUTE to know    │           │ ✅ Static — can READ directly│
  │    desired state           │           │                              │
  │ ❌ Output depends on $ENV  │           │ ✅ Output is deterministic   │
  │    (not in the script!)    │           │    (always 3 replicas)       │
  │ ❌ Can't diff — script     │           │ ✅ Can diff — YAML vs cluster │
  │    is code, not data       │           │    is a pure data comparison  │
  │ ❌ Executing = side effects│           │ ✅ Reading = no side effects │
  │    (changes the cluster!)  │           │    (safe to diff anytime)    │
  └────────────────────────────┘           └────────────────────────────┘

  This is why Declarative is Principle #1: without it, the reconciler is blind.

Imagine anihpj/jobpost's deployment was defined by a Bash script instead of YAML. The reconciler would have to EXECUTE the script to know the desired state — but executing the script CHANGES the cluster. Without declarative config, GitOps is impossible.

GitOps Terminology Q5 of 200
Which of the following is a declarative description of anihpj/jobpost?
A) A Kubernetes Deployment YAML file in Git stating: replicas: 3, image: anihpj-api:v1.2.4, resources: {cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi}, port: 8000 — all versioned and stored in the anihpj-gitops repo
B) A shell script that runs kubectl apply, kubectl scale, and kubectl expose commands in sequence
C) A README.md document describing the deployment architecture and manual steps to deploy
D) The output of "kubectl get all -n anihpj" which shows what is currently running in the cluster
✅ Correct Answer: A

This YAML describes WHAT should exist. It doesn't say "run kubectl apply" — it just declares the desired state. The reconciler (ArgoCD/Flux) does the rest. Options B, C, and D all describe things that are NOT declarative: a script (imperative HOW), documentation (not machine-readable), and current state (not desired state).

📝 Detailed Explanation — The anihpj-api Deployment YAML
Declarative Description of anihpj/jobpost — The Actual YAML in Git
  # File: environments/prod/django-api-deploy.yaml
  # This file IS the declarative desired state for anihpj-api in production.
  # It lives in Git. ArgoCD reads it and ensures the cluster matches.

  apiVersion: apps/v1
  kind: Deployment
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-api
    namespace: anihpj
    labels:
      app: anihpj-api
      environment: production       # ← Declared, not inferred
  spec:
    replicas: 3                     # ← "I want 3" — not "run scale --replicas=3"
    selector:
      matchLabels:
        app: anihpj-api
    template:
      metadata:
        labels:
          app: anihpj-api
          version: v1.2.4
      spec:
        serviceAccountName: anihpj-api-sa  # ← RBAC declared here too
        containers:
        - name: api
          image: anihpj-api@sha256:8a3f4b9c...  # ← Immutable digest!
          ports:
          - containerPort: 8000
          resources:
            requests:
              cpu: "250m"
              memory: "256Mi"
            limits:
              cpu: "500m"           # ← Resources declared
              memory: "512Mi"
          livenessProbe:            # ← Health check declared
            httpGet:
              path: /health/
              port: 8000
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /ready/
              port: 8000

  # Every field above is DECLARATIVE.
  # It says WHAT (3 replicas, 500m CPU, /health/ probe).
  # It does NOT say HOW (no kubectl commands, no scripts).
  # ArgoCD takes this YAML and makes the cluster match it.
🔬 Why Options B, C, and D Are NOT Declarative
The 4 Candidates — Only One Is a Declarative Description
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  IS THIS A DECLARATIVE DESCRIPTION?                                  │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                                                                     │
  │  A) Deployment YAML in Git ✅ CORRECT                                │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ ✅ Machine-readable (YAML is structured data)               │    │
  │  │ ✅ States WHAT (3 replicas, 500m CPU, port 8000)           │    │
  │  │ ✅ Does NOT say HOW (no kubectl commands inside)            │    │
  │  │ ✅ Versioned (Git commit SHA)                               │    │
  │  │ ✅ Diffable (git diff shows what changed)                   │    │
  │  │ ✅ Reconciler can compare without executing                 │    │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                     │
  │  B) Shell Script ❌                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ ❌ Imperative — describes HOW, step by step                 │    │
  │  │ ❌ Must execute to know outcome (dangerous side effects)    │    │
  │  │ ❌ Outcome depends on environment ($ENV vars, current state)│    │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                     │
  │  C) README.md ❌                                                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ ❌ Human-readable, NOT machine-parseable                    │    │
  │  │ ❌ "Step 3: scale to 3 pods" — ArgoCD can't parse English   │    │
  │  │ ❌ Free-form text, no schema validation                     │    │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                     │
  │  D) kubectl get output ❌                                            │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ ❌ Shows ACTUAL state, not DESIRED state                    │    │
  │  │ ❌ Snapshot — what IS, not what SHOULD BE                   │    │
  │  │ ❌ GitOps starts from desired state → reconciles to actual  │    │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Only Option A meets the definition: a machine-readable, versioned,
  diffable statement of WHAT should exist — stored in Git.

For anihpj/jobpost: the environments/prod/ directory holds ~15 such YAML files — each one a declarative description of one piece of the application. Together they form the complete desired state. ArgoCD reads all 15 and ensures the kubeadm cluster matches. 15 YAML files in Git = the entire anihpj/jobpost production system definition.

GitOps Terminology Q6 of 200
What is the relationship between "Continuous" and "Declarative" in GitOps?
A) Declarative defines WHAT. Continuous ensures it STAYS that way. Declarative = the map (destination). Continuous = the GPS that keeps recalculating when you take a wrong turn. They are complementary — neither works without the other
B) Continuous is the older term; Declarative replaced it in the CNCF GitOps specification v2.0
C) They are synonyms — "Continuous" and "Declarative" mean exactly the same thing in GitOps
D) Continuous applies to CI pipelines; Declarative applies to CD pipelines — they operate in different domains
✅ Correct Answer: A

Declarative and Continuous are the yin and yang of GitOps — two inseparable halves that together make the system work. Declarative provides the target. Continuous provides the persistence. Without declarative configs, there's nothing to enforce. Without continuous reconciliation, declarative configs are just "hope-based deployment."

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Map & GPS Analogy
Declarative = Map | Continuous = GPS — Two Halves of One System
  DECLARATIVE (The MAP)                    CONTINUOUS (The GPS)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 🗺️ Shows the DESTINATION   │          │ 📡 Keeps RECALCULATING      │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ "3 replicas of anihpj-api" │          │ "Checking every 3 minutes" │
  │ "image: api@sha256:abc123" │          │ "Still 3 replicas? Check." │
  │ "port: 8000"               │          │ "Still correct image? Ditto"│
  │ "cpu: 500m, mem: 512Mi"    │          │ "Drift? Reroute to target." │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ ✅ Static target            │          │ ✅ Dynamic enforcement      │
  │ ✅ Versioned in Git         │          │ ✅ Infinite loop            │
  │ ✅ Reviewable via PR        │          │ ✅ Self-healing            │
  └──────────┬─────────────────┘          └────────────┬───────────────┘
             │                                         │
             └────────────── WORK TOGETHER ────────────┘
                        ┌─────────────────┐
                        │   GITOPS SYSTEM  │
                        │                  │
                        │  MAP says WHERE  │
                        │  GPS keeps you   │
                        │  ON TRACK 🔄     │
                        └─────────────────┘

  WITHOUT DECLARATIVE: GPS has no destination → Drives in circles.
  WITHOUT CONTINUOUS: Map sits in glovebox → Never gets followed.
  TOGETHER: You always arrive at the declared destination. 🎯
🔬 "Hope-Based Deployment" vs GitOps — Real-World Consequences
What Happens When One Principle Is Missing
  SCENARIO 1: DECLARATIVE ONLY (no Continuous)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ You write beautiful YAML in Git. It's reviewed. It's perfect.  │
  │ Then someone runs: kubectl edit deploy anihpj-api              │
  │ They change replicas: 3 → 1 (by accident).                     │
  │                                                                 │
  │ Result: Git says 3 replicas. Cluster has 1. No one notices.    │
  │ The job board slows down. Users complain.                      │
  │ "But we use GitOps!" — No, you use Git-backed configuration.    │
  │ GitOps requires BOTH: Declarative + Continuous.                 │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SCENARIO 2: CONTINUOUS ONLY (no Declarative)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ You set up ArgoCD. It's polling. It's ready.                   │
  │ But your config is a deploy.sh script in Git — not YAML.       │
  │ ArgoCD can't diff a script against the cluster.                │
  │ It doesn't know what the "desired state" is.                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │ Result: ArgoCD runs but does nothing. Sync status: Unknown.    │
  │ The reconciler is blind — it has no map to compare against.    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  GITOPS = DECLARATIVE + CONTINUOUS. Remove either → It breaks.

For anihpj/jobpost: The Deployment YAML in Git (Declarative) defines the desired state. ArgoCD (Continuous) polls every 3 minutes to verify the cluster matches. If someone runs kubectl edit deploy anihpj-api, ArgoCD detects the drift and reverts it. The map says where to go. The GPS keeps you on track. Both are essential. Neither works alone.

1.2 Desired State & State DriftQ7-Q12

GitOps Terminology Q7 of 200
What is the "Desired State" in GitOps?
A) The configuration stored in the Git repository — it represents EXACTLY what should be running in the cluster. For anihpj/jobpost, the desired state is the YAML files in environments/ that define Deployments, Services, Ingress, ConfigMaps, etc.
B) The current output of "kubectl get all" — whatever is running right now IS the desired state
C) A mental model in the DevOps engineer's head of how the system should behave under ideal conditions
D) The performance metrics dashboard showing target CPU usage, memory consumption, and response times
✅ Correct Answer: A

Desired state = "the truth." It lives in Git. It's versioned (every change = commit SHA), reviewable (via Pull Requests), and auditable (git log shows who changed what and when). Everything else — the actual cluster state — is measured against this truth. If it's not in Git, it doesn't exist.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Blueprint Analogy
Desired State = Blueprint | Cluster = Building | Reconciler = Foreman
  GIT REPOSITORY (Blueprint)           CLUSTER (Building)         RECONCILER (Foreman)
  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────┐     ┌────────────────────┐
  │ environments/prod/     │         │ kubeadm cluster    │     │ ArgoCD             │
  │                        │         │                    │     │                    │
  │ 📄 deploy-api.yaml     │         │ 🏗️ anihpj-api pod  │     │ 👷 Walks the site   │
  │   replicas: 3      ────┼────┐    │   3 pods running   │     │ every 3 minutes:    │
  │   image: api:v1.2.4    │    │    │   v1.2.4 ✅         │     │                    │
  │                        │    │    │                    │     │ "Blueprint says 3   │
  │ 📄 deploy-web.yaml     │    │    │ 🏗️ anihpj-web pod  │     │  replicas...        │
  │   replicas: 2      ────┼────┤    │   2 pods running   │     │  Building has 3.    │
  │                        │    │    │                    │     │  ✅ Match!"         │
  │ 📄 postgres-sts.yaml   │    │    │ 🏗️ anihpj-db pod   │     │                    │
  │   replicas: 1          │    │    │   1 pod running    │     │ "Blueprint says     │
  │                        │    │    │                    │     │  cpu: 500m...       │
  │ 📄 ingress.yaml     ───┼────┘    │ 🏗️ Ingress rules   │     │  Building has       │
  │   TLS: enabled          │         │   TLS active ✅     │     │  500m. ✅ Match!"   │
  │                        │         │                    │     │                    │
  │ 📄 networkpolicy.yaml   │         │ 🏗️ NetPol rules    │     │ 🛠️ "Found a window  │
  │   default-deny          │         │   ❌ MISSING!      │     │   not in blueprint  │
  │                        │         │   (someone deleted) │     │   → FIXING IT!"    │
  └────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────┘     └────────────────────┘

  The blueprint (Git) DEFINES. The building (Cluster) IMPLEMENTS.
  The foreman (ArgoCD) ENFORCES — continuously comparing and correcting.
🔬 The Desired State in anihpj/jobpost — Where It Lives
anihpj-gitops/environments/prod/ — The Complete Desired State
  environments/prod/                         ← Kustomize overlay (prod)
  ├── kustomization.yaml                     ← Lists all resources below
  ├── namespace.yaml                         ← Namespace: anihpj
  ├── postgres-sts.yaml                      ← StatefulSet: PostgreSQL (1 replica, PVC)
  ├── postgres-svc.yaml                      ← ClusterIP: anihpj-db:5432
  ├── redis-deploy.yaml                      ← Deployment: Redis (1 replica)
  ├── redis-svc.yaml                         ← ClusterIP: anihpj-redis:6379
  ├── django-api-deploy.yaml                 ← Deployment: anihpj-api (3 replicas)
  ├── django-api-svc.yaml                    ← ClusterIP: anihpj-api:8000
  ├── django-web-deploy.yaml                 ← Deployment: anihpj-web (2 replicas)
  ├── django-web-svc.yaml                    ← ClusterIP: anihpj-web:80
  ├── ingress.yaml                           ← Ingress: TLS + routing rules
  ├── networkpolicy.yaml                     ← default-deny + allow web→api→db
  ├── hpa.yaml                               ← HPA: api CPU 70%, min 2, max 10
  ├── rbac.yaml                              ← ServiceAccounts + Roles
  └── pdb.yaml                               ← PodDisruptionBudget: minAvailable 2

  These ~15 files = THE DESIRED STATE of anihpj/jobpost in production.
  ArgoCD reads them ALL. Ensures the cluster matches ALL of them.
  If any file changes in Git → ArgoCD syncs. If any resource changes
  in the cluster → ArgoCD reverts. Git always wins.

For anihpj/jobpost: the environments/prod/ directory IS the desired state. Every Deployment, Service, Ingress, NetworkPolicy, HPA, RBAC rule — all declared as YAML in Git. ArgoCD reads these 15 files and ensures the kubeadm cluster matches. If you want to change something, you change Git. There is no other way.

GitOps Terminology Q8 of 200
What is "State Drift" in GitOps?
A) When the ACTUAL state of the cluster diverges from the DESIRED state in Git. Example: Someone runs "kubectl edit deploy anihpj-api" and changes the image tag manually. Now the cluster says v1.2.5 but Git says v1.2.4 — that's drift
B) When the Git repository is out of disk space and can't accept new commits
C) When the Kubernetes cluster nodes drift apart due to network latency between availability zones
D) When two developers make conflicting changes to the same YAML file in Git
✅ Correct Answer: A

State Drift is the enemy of GitOps. It means your Git repo is lying about what's running. The cluster says one thing, Git says another. Drift happens via: manual kubectl commands, auto-scalers changing replica counts, infrastructure failures, or unauthorized access.

📝 Detailed Explanation — How Drift Happens
State Drift — Git Says One Thing, Cluster Says Another
  GIT (Desired State)              CLUSTER (Actual State)           DRIFT?
  ────────────────────             ──────────────────────           ─────
  anihpj-api:                      anihpj-api:
    replicas: 3                      replicas: 5                    ❌ DRIFT!
    image: v1.2.4                    image: v1.2.5                  ❌ DRIFT!
    cpu: 500m                        cpu: 500m                      ✅ MATCH

  anihpj-web:                      anihpj-web:
    replicas: 2                      replicas: 2                    ✅ MATCH
    image: nginx:1.25                image: nginx:1.26              ❌ DRIFT!

  networkpolicy:                   networkpolicy:
    default-deny                     ❌ MISSING! (deleted)          ❌ DRIFT!

  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Common causes of drift:
  😰 Manual kubectl:   kubectl edit deploy anihpj-api (changes image)
  📈 Auto-scaler:      HPA scaled replicas 3→5 (but Git still says 3)
  💥 Incident:         Someone ran kubectl delete netpol during debug
  🔓 Unauthorized:     Compromised ServiceAccount modified resources
  🐛 Bug:              Operator/controller with buggy reconciliation
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
🔬 Drift Is Inevitable — That's Why Continuous Reconciliation Exists
Drift Timeline — Detection → Correction → Prevention
  TIME   EVENT                                    GIT       CLUSTER    STATUS
  ────   ──────────────────────────────────────   ────────  ────────   ──────
  T0     Initial sync from Git                    v1.2.4    v1.2.4     Synced ✅
  T1     Developer runs:                          v1.2.4    v1.2.5     DRIFT ❌
         kubectl edit deploy anihpj-api
         (changes image tag to v1.2.5)
  T2     ArgoCD polls (next 3-min cycle)          v1.2.4    v1.2.5     OutOfSync
  T3     ArgoCD 3-way diff:                       v1.2.4    v1.2.5     OutOfSync
         Git:v1.2.4 ≠ Cluster:v1.2.5 → DRIFT
  T4     ArgoCD auto-sync: reverts to v1.2.4     v1.2.4    v1.2.4     Synced ✅
  ────   ──────────────────────────────────────   ────────  ────────   ──────
  Result: Drift lasted ~3 minutes. ArgoCD auto-corrected.
  Without GitOps: Drift is PERMANENT until a human notices and fixes.

For anihpj/jobpost: Drift is not a theoretical problem — it happens. A well-meaning colleague runs kubectl edit deploy anihpj-api to "quickly fix" something. Now the Git repo is lying. ArgoCD catches this within 3 minutes and reverts the manual edit. Git is the source of truth — always.

GitOps Terminology Q9 of 200
How does a GitOps reconciler detect State Drift?
A) The reconciler periodically compares the manifests in Git against the live resources in the cluster via the Kubernetes API. ArgoCD uses a 3-way diff: (1) desired state in Git, (2) live state in cluster, (3) last synced state. If ANY field differs — it's drift
B) The reconciler sends a daily email to the DevOps team asking them to manually verify each resource
C) The reconciler monitors Slack channels for messages like "hey, did someone change the deployment?"
D) The reconciler compares the Git commit timestamps with the pod creation timestamps — if they don't match within 5 minutes, it's drift
✅ Correct Answer: A

ArgoCD's 3-way diff is the secret sauce of drift detection. It doesn't just compare Git vs Cluster (2-way) — it compares Git vs Cluster vs Last-Synced. This 3-way comparison can tell the difference between "someone changed Git" and "someone changed the cluster" — even when both changed simultaneously.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The 3-Way Diff Algorithm
3-Way Diff — How ArgoCD Knows Exactly What Changed
  2-WAY DIFF (naive)                        3-WAY DIFF (ArgoCD)
  ┌──────────────────────┐                 ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ Git ────DIFF──── Cluster│               │ Git ──┐                      │
  │                        │               │       ├── DIFF ──► Combined │
  │ "Something changed!"   │               │ Last ─┘    (merge)    Result │
  │ But WHAT changed?      │               │        │                      │
  │ And WHO changed it?    │               │ Cluster┘                      │
  │ And WHY?               │               │                               │
  │                        │               │ "Git changed field X"         │
  │ ❌ Can't distinguish:  │               │ "Cluster changed field Y"     │
  │   Git edit vs          │               │ "Both changed field Z"        │
  │   Cluster edit vs      │               │                               │
  │   Both changed         │               │ ✅ Full attribution           │
  └──────────────────────┘                 └──────────────────────────────┘

  EXAMPLE: anihpj-api Deployment
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     replicas   image        cpu                 │
  │  Git (desired):        3       v1.2.4      500m                 │
  │  Last-Synced:          3       v1.2.3      500m                 │
  │  Cluster (live):       5       v1.2.4      500m                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │  3-WAY ANALYSIS:                                                │
  │  • replicas: Git=3, Last=3, Cluster=5                           │
  │    → Cluster changed from 3→5 (someone scaled manually!)        │
  │  • image: Git=v1.2.4, Last=v1.2.3, Cluster=v1.2.4              │
  │    → Git changed from v1.2.3→v1.2.4 (expected deploy!)         │
  │  • cpu: Git=500m, Last=500m, Cluster=500m                       │
  │    → No change — MATCH                                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔬 The Reconciliation Cycle — From Poll to Fix
Detection → Decision → Action — The Full Drift Handling Pipeline
  STEP 1: FETCH                        STEP 2: DIFF
  ┌──────────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ ArgoCD git-fetch     │            │ 3-way merge:                 │
  │ (every 3 min /       │──────────▶ │ Git vs Live vs Last-Synced   │
  │  webhook trigger)    │            │                              │
  │                      │            │ MATCH? → Skip, report Synced │
  │ Renders manifests:   │            │ DIFF?  → Go to STEP 3        │
  │ Kustomize / Helm →   │            └──────────────┬───────────────┘
  │ plain K8s objects    │                           │
  └──────────────────────┘                           ▼
                                            ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  STEP 4: REPORT                           │ STEP 3: DECIDE                │
  ┌──────────────────────────┐            │                              │
  │ Sync Status: Synced ✅    │            │ Auto-Sync ON?                │
  │ Health: Healthy ✅        │◀───────────│   YES → Apply changes        │
  │                          │            │   NO  → Report OutOfSync     │
  │ Notifications (Slack):   │            │         (wait for human)     │
  │ "anihpj-prod: Synced ✅" │            │                              │
  └──────────────────────────┘            │ Pruning ON?                  │
                                           │   YES → Delete orphaned objs │
                                           │   NO  → Keep extra resources │
                                           └──────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: ArgoCD's 3-way diff runs every 3 minutes against the kubeadm cluster. It compares the rendered YAML from Git against the live Kubernetes objects. If someone changed the image tag via kubectl edit, ArgoCD sees: Git=v1.2.4, Last-Synced=v1.2.4, Cluster=v1.2.5 → DRIFT DETECTED. The reconciler knows exactly what changed, who likely changed it (cluster-side edit), and what action to take (revert to Git).

GitOps Terminology Q10 of 200
What happens when the GitOps reconciler detects State Drift in anihpj/jobpost?
A) By default, the reconciler CORRECTS the drift — it reverts the cluster to match Git. If someone manually scaled anihpj-api to 10 replicas, ArgoCD scales it back to 3. This is "self-healing" or "drift correction" — Git always wins
B) The reconciler sends an email to the DevOps team and waits for manual approval before making any changes
C) The reconciler accepts the cluster state as the new desired state and updates Git to match the cluster
D) The reconciler rolls back ALL deployments in the namespace to the last known good state, regardless of which specific resource drifted
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is the "Continuously Reconciled" principle in action. The reconciler is the enforcer — Git always wins. When drift is detected, the reconciler doesn't ask permission, doesn't send an email, doesn't update Git to match the cluster. It forcefully reverts the cluster to match Git. This is self-healing.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Self-Healing in Action
Self-Healing Timeline — Manual Scale → Drift → Auto-Correct
  TIME   ACTION                                          REPLICAS   STATUS
  ────   ────────────────────────────────────────────    ────────   ──────
  T0     Git says: replicas: 3                             3       Synced ✅
         Cluster has: 3 pods running                       3
  ────   ────────────────────────────────────────────    ────────   ──────
  T1     ALICE: "Hmm, the API seems slow...                3       Synced ✅
         Let me scale it up real quick."                   3
         $ kubectl scale deploy anihpj-api                 3
           --replicas=10 -n anihpj                        10       ⚠️ DRIFT
  ────   ────────────────────────────────────────────    ────────   ──────
  T2     ARGOCD POLLS (3 min later)                       10       OutOfSync
         Git: replicas=3                                  10
         Cluster: replicas=10                             10
         3-way diff → DRIFT DETECTED!                     10
  ────   ────────────────────────────────────────────    ────────   ──────
  T3     ARGOCD AUTO-SYNCS                                10→3     Progressing
         Applies Git state: replicas=3                     3
         Terminates 7 extra pods (graceful shutdown)       3
  ────   ────────────────────────────────────────────    ────────   ──────
  T4     Health checks pass                                3       Synced ✅
         3 pods Running + Ready                            3
         ArgoCD: "Synced + Healthy ✅"                     3
  ────   ────────────────────────────────────────────    ────────   ──────

  TOTAL DRIFT DURATION: ~3 minutes (from manual scale to auto-correct)
  WITHOUT GITOPS: 10 replicas run FOREVER (wasting resources, costing $$)
🔬 Auto-Sync ON vs OFF — When Self-Healing Is Disabled
Two Sync Modes — Auto-Sync (Default) vs Manual Sync
  AUTO-SYNC: ON (Default — ArgoCD)         AUTO-SYNC: OFF
  ┌────────────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Drift detected →              │      │ Drift detected →              │
  │   Automatically REVERT        │      │   Report OutOfSync (yellow)   │
  │   Cluster → Git               │      │   DO NOT change cluster        │
  │                               │      │                                │
  │ ✅ Self-healing                │      │ ❌ No self-healing             │
  │ ✅ Fast recovery (~3 min)      │      │ ❌ Human must click "Sync"    │
  │ ✅ No human needed             │      │ ❌ Drift persists until action │
  │                               │      │                                │
  │ Use for: Production, staging  │      │ Use for: Incident response,   │
  │ (Git IS the control plane)    │      │ debugging (freeze cluster     │
  │                               │      │ while investigating)          │
  │ ⚠️ Risk: Accidental Git change │      │ ⚠️ Risk: Drift goes unnoticed │
  │   → instantly propagated      │      │   for hours/days              │
  └────────────────────────────────┘      └────────────────────────────────┘

  BEST PRACTICE: Auto-sync ON with PR reviews protecting the Git branch.
  The PR review prevents bad changes from reaching Git.
  Auto-sync prevents manual changes from persisting in the cluster.
  Together = defense-in-depth for configuration.

For anihpj/jobpost: Auto-sync is enabled on the kubeadm cluster. If Alice manually scales anihpj-api to 10 replicas, ArgoCD reverts it to 3 within 3 minutes — saving compute resources and ensuring consistency. If the team actually NEEDS 5 replicas, they must update the Deployment YAML in Git and go through PR review. There is no shortcut. Git is the control plane. The reconciler is the enforcer.

GitOps Terminology Q11 of 200
What is the difference between "OutOfSync" and "Degraded" in ArgoCD?
A) OutOfSync = the manifest in Git doesn't match the live resource (drift detected). Degraded = the live resource is unhealthy (e.g., pod CrashLoopBackOff). An app can be Synced but Degraded — the YAML matches but the pods are crashing
B) OutOfSync means the cluster is down; Degraded means the Git repo is unreachable
C) They are synonyms — both indicate the same problem with different severity levels
D) OutOfSync applies to Deployments; Degraded applies only to StatefulSets
✅ Correct Answer: A

Sync status = configuration accuracy. Health status = runtime health. Both matter: you want Synced + Healthy. An app that's Synced but Degraded means your YAML is correct but your application code has a bug — ArgoCD deployed it correctly, but the application itself is broken.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Two Dimensions of App Status
Sync Status vs Health Status — 4 Possible Combinations
                     SYNC STATUS
                     Synced ✅          OutOfSync ❌
           ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  H   ✅   │ 🟢 Synced + Healthy       🟡 OutOfSync + Healthy  │
  E   H   │ "Everything perfect"       "Git was updated,       │
  A   E   │ Git = Cluster = Running    waiting for sync"       │
  L   A   │                            (normal during deploy)  │
  T   L   ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  H   T   │                            🔴 OutOfSync + Degraded│
      H   │ 🟠 Synced + Degraded       "Manual kubectl broke  │
  S   ❌   │ "Config is right,          something AND app is   │
  T        │  but app is crashing"      crashing — double fail"│
  A        │ (bug in code, not config)                         │
  T   ─   └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  U
  S        anihpj/jobpost examples:
           🟢 Synced+Healthy:   Normal operation
           🟡 OutOfSync+Healthy: Rolling update in progress (new pods spinning up)
           🟠 Synced+Degraded:  anihpj-api pods CrashLoopBackOff (bad image)
           🔴 OutOfSync+Degraded: Someone kubectl-edited + pods crashing
🔬 How ArgoCD Determines Health Status
Health Assessment — ArgoCD Checks Each Resource Type
  RESOURCE TYPE       HEALTHY IF...                     DEGRADED IF...
  ───────────────     ─────────────────────────────     ──────────────────────
  Deployment          AvailableReplicas ≥ 1 AND          AvailableReplicas = 0
                      ObservedGeneration = Generation    (no pods available)
  StatefulSet         CurrentReplicas = Replicas         CurrentReplicas ≠ Replicas
  DaemonSet           NumberReady = DesiredNumber        NumberReady = 0
  Service             Endpoints have ready addresses     No endpoints
  Ingress             Status.loadBalancer is set         No load balancer assigned
  PersistentVolume    Status.Phase = Bound               Status.Phase = Failed
  ───────────────     ─────────────────────────────     ──────────────────────

  For anihpj/jobpost: Synced means the 15 YAML files match the cluster.
  Healthy means every pod is Running + Ready, every Service has endpoints,
  the Ingress is accepting traffic, and the PVC is Bound.

For anihpj/jobpost: You might see 🟠 Synced + Degraded if someone deploys a broken Docker image (v1.2.5 has a bug → pods crash). ArgoCD synced the YAML correctly, but the application is unhealthy. The fix: revert the image tag in Git — not kubectl. Sync status tells you about configuration. Health status tells you about runtime. You need both.

GitOps Terminology Q12 of 200
Why does GitOps consider manual kubectl commands as State Drift?
A) Because manual kubectl commands bypass Git — the source of truth. If you make a change via kubectl, that change is NOT in Git, NOT versioned, NOT reviewed, and NOT reproducible. GitOps treats ANY deviation from Git as drift, including "helpful" manual fixes
B) Because kubectl is deprecated and will be removed in future Kubernetes versions
C) Because kubectl commands are slower than GitOps sync operations
D) Manual kubectl commands are not drift — they are an accepted way to make emergency changes in GitOps
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is a cultural shift — not just a technical rule. In GitOps, the ONLY way to change the cluster is to change Git. If something is broken, you fix it in Git (and optionally accelerate the sync), not via kubectl. "Git is the control plane." Every kubectl command that changes cluster state is a deviation from the source of truth.

📝 Detailed Explanation — kubectl = Bypassing the Control Plane
Why kubectl Creates Drift — The Chain of Trust
  GITOPS WAY (Trusted)                    KUBECTL WAY (Untrusted)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① Developer edits YAML     │         │ ① Someone runs:            │
  │    in Git                   │         │    kubectl edit deploy     │
  │ ② PR review → peer approves│         │    (changes replicas: 3→10)│
  │ ③ Merge to main branch     │         │                            │
  │ ④ ArgoCD detects change    │         │ ❌ No Git history           │
  │ ⑤ ArgoCD syncs to cluster  │         │ ❌ No PR review             │
  │ ⑥ Git commit = audit trail │         │ ❌ No approval              │
  │                            │         │ ❌ Not reproducible         │
  │ ✅ WHO: git log shows author│         │ ❌ "Who did this?" unknown  │
  │ ✅ WHAT: git diff shows Δ  │         │ ❌ "Why?" — no commit msg   │
  │ ✅ WHEN: git log timestamp │         │ ❌ Can't rollback via Git   │
  │ ✅ WHY: commit message     │         │                            │
  └────────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────────────┘

  The kubectl change is INVISIBLE to the GitOps system — until ArgoCD
  detects the drift and REVERTS it. The reconciler treats manual kubectl
  as an unauthorized change and corrects it.
🔬 "Git is the Control Plane" — What This Really Means
Control Plane Analogy — Git ↔ kubectl
  TRADITIONAL K8s                          GITOPS K8s
  ┌────────────────────────┐             ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Control Plane:         │             │ Control Plane:             │
  │   API Server (kubectl) │             │   GIT REPOSITORY           │
  │                        │             │                            │
  │ "kubectl apply" =      │             │ "git push" =               │
  │   direct command        │             │   change request           │
  │                        │             │                            │
  │ No audit beyond        │             │ Full audit: commit SHA,    │
  │   API audit logs       │             │ author, timestamp, diff,   │
  │                        │             │ PR review, CI checks       │
  └────────────────────────┘             └────────────────────────────┘

  In GitOps, kubectl is a READ-ONLY tool (get, describe, logs, top).
  kubectl is for OBSERVING the cluster, not CHANGING it.
  To change: git commit → git push → ArgoCD sync.

For anihpj/jobpost: Even during an incident at 3 AM, the on-call engineer should NOT run kubectl to fix things. Instead: (1) Diagnose via kubectl get/describe/logs (read-only), (2) Edit the YAML in Git, (3) Push and trigger immediate sync via ArgoCD webhook or `argocd app sync`. The fix is in Git, reviewed post-incident, and auditable forever. Emergency is not an excuse to bypass Git.

1.3 State ReconciliationQ13-Q17

GitOps Terminology Q13 of 200
What is "State Reconciliation" in GitOps?
A) The process where the reconciler continuously compares desired state (Git) with actual state (cluster) and takes action to eliminate differences — creating, updating, or deleting resources as needed. It loops forever: Observe → Diff → Act → Repeat
B) The process of merging two conflicting Git branches into a single branch
C) A one-time operation that aligns the cluster with Git when ArgoCD is first installed
D) The process of reconciling financial billing data between cloud providers and on-premise infrastructure
✅ Correct Answer: A

Reconciliation = the engine of GitOps. It's a loop: Observe (get cluster state) → Diff (compare with Git) → Act (apply changes) → Repeat. The reconciler doesn't just run once — it loops forever. Analogy: A Roomba vacuum that keeps cleaning until the floor matches the "clean" desired state.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Reconciliation Loop
The Roomba Analogy — Continuous Reconciliation
  ROOMBA (Reconciler)                      GITOPS RECONCILER (ArgoCD)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① SENSORS: Scan the floor  │          │ ① GET: Fetch cluster state  │
  │    "Where's the dirt?"     │          │    via Kubernetes API       │
  │                            │          │                             │
  │ ② COMPARE: Is floor clean? │          │ ② DIFF: Git desired vs      │
  │    Clean map vs actual     │          │    Cluster actual vs        │
  │                            │          │    Last-synced state        │
  │ ③ ACT: Vacuum the dirt     │          │                             │
  │    Move to dirty spot      │          │ ③ ACT: Create/Update/Delete │
  │                            │          │    resources as needed      │
  │ ④ REPEAT: Keep scanning    │          │                             │
  │    New dirt? → Vacuum again│          │ ④ REPEAT: Poll again in     │
  │                            │          │    3 minutes (or webhook)   │
  │ 🔄 NEVER STOPS             │          │ 🔄 NEVER STOPS              │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘

  The Roomba doesn't clean ONCE and say "done" — it keeps checking.
  ArgoCD doesn't sync ONCE and say "done" — it keeps reconciling.
🔬 Reconciliation Actions — Create, Update, Delete
Three Reconciliation Actions — What ArgoCD Does
  GIT CHANGE              RECONCILER ACTION              CLUSTER RESULT
  ─────────────────────   ────────────────────────────   ─────────────────
  NEW file added to Git   → CREATE resource in cluster   → Resource appears
  EXISTING file modified  → UPDATE resource in cluster   → Resource changes
  FILE deleted from Git   → DELETE resource from cluster → Resource removed
                            (if pruning enabled)           (self-cleaning)
  NO change in Git        → SKIP (nothing to do)         → Cluster unchanged

  Example: anihpj/jobpost
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Git: Add hpa.yaml → ArgoCD CREATEs HPA resource in cluster     │
  │ Git: Edit replicas 3→5 → ArgoCD UPDATEs Deployment             │
  │ Git: Delete old-config.yaml → ArgoCD DELETEs ConfigMap         │
  │ Git: No changes → ArgoCD SKIPs (reports Synced + Healthy)      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every 3 minutes, ArgoCD reconciles all 15+ resources in the anihpj namespace. It compares each one against the Git desired state. If anything is missing, extra, or different — ArgoCD fixes it. This is the reconciliation loop that makes GitOps self-healing.

GitOps Terminology Q14 of 200
How often does reconciliation happen in ArgoCD?
A) Default: every 3 minutes (180 seconds) via polling. Can also be triggered instantly via webhooks from Git (GitHub/GitLab → ArgoCD API). Configurable per application with different intervals
B) Once per day at midnight UTC — batch reconciliation for all applications
C) Only when a developer manually clicks the "Sync" button in the ArgoCD dashboard
D) Continuously in real-time — ArgoCD maintains a persistent WebSocket connection to Git for instant updates
✅ Correct Answer: A

The 3-minute default means after you merge a PR, it can take up to 3 minutes for ArgoCD to detect the change (unless you use webhooks). Webhooks provide near-instant reconciliation — Git pushes an HTTP notification to ArgoCD, triggering an immediate sync.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Polling vs Webhooks
Two Trigger Mechanisms — Polling (Default) vs Webhook (Fast)
  POLLING (Default: 3 min)                  WEBHOOK (Near-Instant)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ ArgoCD: "Any changes?"     │           │ Git push → HTTP POST to    │
  │   ↓ every 180 seconds      │           │ ArgoCD webhook endpoint    │
  │ Git: "Nope." / "Yes!"      │           │                            │
  │                             │           │ ArgoCD: "Wake up! Sync    │
  │ ⏱️ Latency: 0-180 seconds   │           │   NOW!"                    │
  │ ✅ Simple, no external deps │           │                            │
  │ ✅ Works air-gapped         │           │ ⏱️ Latency: < 1 second      │
  │ ❌ Up to 3 min delay        │           │ ✅ Near-instant deploys     │
  └────────────────────────────┘           │ ❌ Requires network access  │
                                            │   to ArgoCD from Git      │
  DEFAULT FOR ANIHPJ:                      └────────────────────────────┘
  • Production (kubeadm): Webhooks enabled (fast deploys)
  • Staging (kind): Polling 3 min (OK for testing)
  • Dev (minikube): Polling 1 min (faster feedback)
🔬 Configuring Sync Intervals Per Application
ArgoCD Application Sync Policy Configuration
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Application
  spec:
    syncPolicy:
      automated:
        prune: true              # Delete resources removed from Git
        selfHeal: true           # Auto-correct drift
      syncOptions:
      - CreateNamespace=true
      retry:
        limit: 5                 # Retry failed syncs 5 times
        backoff:
          duration: 5s           # Initial backoff
          factor: 2              # Exponential backoff (5s, 10s, 20s...)
          maxDuration: 3m        # Max backoff

    # POLLING INTERVAL (override default 3 min):
    # Not in the Application spec — configured via ArgoCD configmap:
    # kubectl edit cm argocd-cm -n argocd
    #   data:
    #     timeout.reconciliation: 180s   ← Default: 180s (3 min)

For anihpj/jobpost: Production uses webhooks for near-instant deploys. When a developer merges a PR to the prod branch, GitHub sends a webhook to ArgoCD, and the new version is deployed within seconds. The 3-minute polling still runs as a fallback — if the webhook fails, polling catches the change within 3 minutes.

GitOps Terminology Q15 of 200
What does ArgoCD do during reconciliation when it finds a NEW resource in Git?
A) It CREATES that resource in the cluster automatically — no human runs kubectl. You add a YAML file to Git → ArgoCD detects it → ArgoCD creates it in the cluster
B) It sends a notification to the DevOps team asking them to manually run kubectl apply
C) It adds the resource to a queue and creates it during the next scheduled maintenance window
D) It ignores the new resource until the application is manually re-synced from the ArgoCD dashboard
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is how GitOps deploys things: Git push → wait → deployed. The entire deployment process is automated. You add a YAML file to Git. ArgoCD detects it within 3 minutes (or instantly via webhook). ArgoCD creates the resource in the cluster. No human touches kubectl.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Create Flow
New Resource Creation — From Git File to Running Pod
  STEP 1: Developer creates           STEP 4: ArgoCD applies
  ┌────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Add hpa.yaml to Git:   │         │ kubectl apply -f hpa.yaml  │
  │ environments/prod/     │         │ (via Kubernetes API)       │
  │   └── hpa.yaml (NEW!)  │         │                            │
  └───────────┬────────────┘         │ HPA resource created in    │
              │                       │ namespace anihpj           │
              ▼                       └───────────┬────────────────┘
  STEP 2: git commit + push                      │
  ┌────────────────────────┐                      ▼
  │ git add hpa.yaml       │         STEP 5: Verification
  │ git commit -m "add HPA"│         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ git push origin main   │         │ ArgoCD checks:             │
  └───────────┬────────────┘         │ Synced ✅ + Healthy ✅      │
              │                       │                            │
              ▼                       │ New HPA is active —        │
  STEP 3: ArgoCD detects              │ anihpj-api now auto-scales │
  ┌────────────────────────┐         └────────────────────────────┘
  │ 3-way diff finds:      │
  │ Git: hpa.yaml (NEW)    │         ⏱️ Total time: ~3-4 minutes
  │ Cluster: (nothing)     │         (poll-based; < 10s with webhook)
  │ Action: CREATE         │
  └────────────────────────┘
🔬 What Happens If Creation Fails?
Sync Failure — Retry & Error Handling
  SYNC ATTEMPT 1 (FAILS)               RETRY BACKOFF
  ┌────────────────────────────┐      ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Git → ArgoCD: CREATE HPA  │      │ Attempt 1: FAIL (t=0)      │
  │                            │      │   Wait 5s...               │
  │ ❌ ERROR: RBAC denied —    │      │ Attempt 2: FAIL (t=5s)     │
  │    ServiceAccount lacks    │      │   Wait 10s...              │
  │    permissions to create   │      │ Attempt 3: FAIL (t=15s)    │
  │    HPA resources           │      │   Wait 20s...              │
  │                            │      │ Attempt 4: FAIL (t=35s)    │
  │ Status: OutOfSync ❌        │      │   Wait 40s...              │
  │ Error visible in ArgoCD UI │      │ Attempt 5: FAIL (t=75s)    │
  └────────────────────────────┘      │   GIVE UP — OutOfSync ❌    │
                                       │                            │
  FIX: Fix the RBAC in Git,           │ Exponential backoff:        │
  not by granting permissions         │ 5s → 10s → 20s → 40s → 80s │
  manually!                            └────────────────────────────┘

If ArgoCD can't create the resource, it retries up to 5 times with exponential backoff. The Git commit is NOT rolled back — the error must be fixed by a NEW commit to Git. Git is always the starting point.

For anihpj/jobpost: Adding a new HPA YAML to environments/prod/ triggers ArgoCD to create the HPA resource in the kubeadm cluster. Within 3 minutes, anihpj-api has auto-scaling enabled — no kubectl, no manual steps, no dashboard clicks.

GitOps Terminology Q16 of 200
What does ArgoCD do when a resource is REMOVED from Git (with pruning enabled)?
A) It DELETES that resource from the cluster. If you delete anihpj-redis-deploy.yaml from Git, ArgoCD deletes the Redis Deployment. This is "pruning" — self-cleaning that prevents orphan resource accumulation
B) It keeps the resource running in the cluster indefinitely as a safety measure
C) It renames the resource with a "deprecated-" prefix and stops syncing it
D) It moves the resource to a "trash" namespace for 30 days before permanent deletion
✅ Correct Answer: A

Pruning = self-cleaning. It ensures the cluster doesn't accumulate orphan resources — resources that exist in the cluster but not in Git. ⚠️ Pruning can be DANGEROUS: if you accidentally delete a PVC YAML from Git, ArgoCD will delete the PVC (and potentially the underlying PV and its data!). Always review diffs before syncing.
