📝 Detailed Explanation — Pruning in Action
Pruning — Git Deletion → Cluster Deletion
  WITHOUT PRUNING                           WITH PRUNING (ArgoCD default)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Git: Delete old-config.yaml│           │ Git: Delete old-config.yaml│
  │                            │           │                            │
  │ ArgoCD: "old-config.yaml   │           │ ArgoCD: "old-config.yaml   │
  │ is gone from Git."         │           │ is gone from Git → DELETE  │
  │                            │           │ the ConfigMap too!"        │
  │ Action: NOTHING            │           │                            │
  │                            │           │ Action: kubectl delete     │
  │ ❌ ConfigMap still exists   │           │   configmap old-config     │
  │ ❌ Cluster has "orphan"     │           │                            │
  │    resources               │           │ ✅ Cluster stays clean      │
  │ ❌ Manual cleanup needed   │           │ ✅ No orphan resources      │
  └────────────────────────────┘           └────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️  PRUNING DANGER ZONE:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ If you delete a PVC YAML from Git WITH pruning enabled:         │
  │   Git: pvc.yaml GONE → ArgoCD: DELETE PVC → PV Released →      │
  │   reclaimPolicy: Delete → EBS volume DESTROYED → DATA LOST!    │
  │                                                                 │
  │ PROTECTION: Use reclaimPolicy: Retain on PVs, and ALWAYS        │
  │ review the ArgoCD diff before syncing when files are deleted.   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔬 Pruning Safety — Best Practices for anihpj/jobpost
Pruning Decision Flow — Is It Safe to Delete?
  You deleted a YAML file from Git
               │
               ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────┐
  │ Is pruning ENABLED?         │
  └────────┬────────────────────┘
           │ YES                    │ NO
           ▼                        ▼
  ┌────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │ Does the resource   │    │ ✅ SAFE — Resource stays │
  │ have DATA?          │    │ in cluster (manual      │
  │ (PVC, PV, DB, etc.) │    │ cleanup needed later)   │
  └────────┬───────────┘    └─────────────────────────┘
           │ YES              │ NO (ConfigMap, Service...)
           ▼                  ▼
  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ ⚠️ DANGER!       │  │ ✅ SAFE — Resource will be │
  │ Review reclaimPolicy│  │ auto-deleted from cluster │
  │ If Delete → DATA   │  │ (self-cleaning works)     │
  │ WILL BE DESTROYED │  └────────────────────────────┘
  │ If Retain → PV     │
  │ survives (cleanup  │
  │ needed)            │
  └────────────────────┘

  GOLDEN RULE: Always check the ArgoCD diff BEFORE syncing deletions!

① Always check the diff before syncing. ArgoCD shows exactly which resources will be deleted. ② Use reclaimPolicy: Retain for critical PVs. ③ Git revert, don't delete — if you accidentally delete a file, git revert the deletion commit.

For anihpj/jobpost: Pruning is enabled on kubeadm. When a resource is intentionally removed (e.g., decommissioning an old microservice), deleting its YAML from Git triggers ArgoCD to clean up the cluster automatically. No manual kubectl delete needed. The Git repo is the single source of truth for what SHOULD exist — and what SHOULDN'T.

GitOps Terminology Q17 of 200
What is a "Sync Wave" in ArgoCD and why is it needed?
A) Sync Waves control the ORDER of resource application. Wave 0 first, then Wave 1, etc. This ensures dependencies are created before dependents — Namespace before Deployment, CRD before Custom Resource, DB before API
B) A Sync Wave is a burst of multiple simultaneous sync operations to speed up deployments
C) A Sync Wave is a notification sent to Slack when a sync completes successfully
D) A Sync Wave is a temporary pause in reconciliation during peak traffic hours
✅ Correct Answer: A

Without sync waves, ArgoCD applies all manifests in parallel, which can cause failures — a Deployment references a ServiceAccount that doesn't exist yet, or a Custom Resource references a CRD that hasn't been created. Sync waves = deployment orchestration within GitOps.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Sync Waves for anihpj/jobpost
Sync Wave Ordering — anihpj/jobpost Deployment Sequence
  WAVE 0: Foundation                    WAVE 2: Stateful Services
  ┌────────────────────────────┐       ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ namespace.yaml             │       │ postgres-sts.yaml          │
  │ (Namespace: anihpj)        │       │ postgres-svc.yaml          │
  │                            │       │ (DB must exist before API) │
  │ argocd.argoproj.io/        │       │                            │
  │   sync-wave: "0"           │       │ sync-wave: "2"             │
  └───────────┬────────────────┘       └───────────┬────────────────┘
              │ APPLIED FIRST                       │ APPLIED THIRD
              ▼                                     ▼
  WAVE 1: Config & Auth                WAVE 3: Application
  ┌────────────────────────────┐       ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ secrets.yaml               │       │ django-api-deploy.yaml     │
  │ configmap.yaml             │       │ django-web-deploy.yaml     │
  │ rbac.yaml                  │       │ redis-deploy.yaml          │
  │ (Secrets must exist        │       │ (Apps need DB + Config     │
  │  before pods reference)    │       │  to exist first)           │
  │                            │       │                            │
  │ sync-wave: "1"             │       │ sync-wave: "3"             │
  └────────────────────────────┘       └───────────┬────────────────┘
                                                     │
  WAVE 4: Networking                               APPLIED LAST
  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ networkpolicy.yaml         │
  │ ingress.yaml               │
  │ hpa.yaml                   │
  │                            │
  │ sync-wave: "4"             │
  └────────────────────────────┘

  RESULT: No "ServiceAccount not found" errors. No race conditions.
  Everything deploys in the correct dependency order.
🔬 How to Set Sync Waves — Annotation Example
Sync Wave Annotation — Applied Per Resource
  # Adding sync-wave annotation to any Kubernetes resource:
  metadata:
    annotations:
      argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "3"   ← Applied in Wave 3

  # Wave ordering example:
  Wave  -1:  Pre-hooks (run BEFORE everything)
  Wave   0:  Namespace, CRDs                  ← Foundation
  Wave   1:  Secrets, ConfigMaps, RBAC        ← Configuration
  Wave   2:  PostgreSQL, Redis, Services      ← Dependencies
  Wave   3:  anihpj-api, anihpj-web           ← Application
  Wave   4:  Ingress, NetworkPolicy, HPA      ← Networking
  Wave  99:  Post-hooks (run AFTER everything)

  # Resources within the SAME wave are applied in PARALLEL.
  # Negative waves run first, then 0, 1, 2, 3...

Add the annotation to any resource: argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "3". ArgoCD applies all Wave 0 resources first, waits for them to be healthy, then Wave 1, etc. Resources within the same wave are applied in parallel.

For anihpj/jobpost: Without sync waves, the anihpj-api Deployment might be created before the PostgreSQL Service exists — causing the API pods to crash with "could not resolve host anihpj-db." With sync waves, the DB starts first (Wave 2), then the API (Wave 3). Order matters in distributed systems. Sync waves enforce it.

1.4 GitOps Managed Software System & State StoreQ18-Q22

GitOps Terminology Q18 of 200
What is a "GitOps Managed Software System"?
A) The entire application and its infrastructure managed via GitOps. For anihpj/jobpost: Django app + PostgreSQL + Redis + Ingress + NetworkPolicies + HPA + RBAC — everything defined in Git and reconciled to the cluster. The "system" = code + config + infrastructure, all Git-managed
B) A specific software tool (like ArgoCD or Flux) that performs GitOps reconciliation
C) The Git repository itself — the ".git" folder that stores version history
D) A Kubernetes operator that manages software licenses for GitOps tools
✅ Correct Answer: A

A GitOps Managed Software System is the complete set of resources that together deliver a working application — not just the Deployment, but the namespace, secrets, services, ingress, network policies, HPA, PDB, monitoring configs, and more. If any piece is managed OUTSIDE Git, the system is only PARTIALLY GitOps-managed.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Full System
anihpj/jobpost — The Complete GitOps Managed Software System
  THE SYSTEM = Everything needed to run anihpj/jobpost in production
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    GITOPS MANAGED (in Git)                       │
  │                                                                 │
  │  🏷️  Namespace: anihpj                                          │
  │  🔐 Secrets: DB password, Django SECRET_KEY, Redis password     │
  │  ⚙️  ConfigMaps: DB host, Redis URL, ALLOWED_HOSTS              │
  │  🗄️  StatefulSet: PostgreSQL 15 (1 replica, 10Gi PVC)          │
  │  🌐 Service: anihpj-db:5432 (ClusterIP)                        │
  │  📦 Deployment: Redis 7-alpine (1 replica)                     │
  │  🌐 Service: anihpj-redis:6379 (ClusterIP)                     │
  │  🐍 Deployment: anihpj-api (3 replicas, gunicorn)              │
  │  🌐 Service: anihpj-api:8000 (ClusterIP)                       │
  │  🌍 Deployment: anihpj-web (2 replicas, nginx)                 │
  │  🌐 Service: anihpj-web:80 (ClusterIP)                         │
  │  🚪 Ingress: TLS + routing (jobpost.com → web, api.jobpost.com)│
  │  🛡️  NetworkPolicy: default-deny + allow web→api→db→redis      │
  │  📈 HPA: anihpj-api (CPU 70%, min 2, max 10)                  │
  │  👤 RBAC: ServiceAccounts + Roles (least privilege)            │
  │  🛑 PDB: minAvailable 2                                        │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ─── 15 resources, ALL in Git, ALL reconciled by ArgoCD ───    │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔬 Partial GitOps = Risk — The Missing Resource Problem
Full GitOps vs Partial GitOps — Risk Comparison
  FULL GITOPS (15/15 resources in Git)    PARTIAL GITOPS (14/15 in Git)
  ┌────────────────────────────────┐     ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │ All resources versioned ✅      │     │ 14 resources in Git ✅         │
  │ All changes auditable ✅        │     │ 1 Ingress created by kubectl ❌ │
  │ Self-healing on everything ✅   │     │                                │
  │ Disaster recovery from Git ✅   │     │ ❌ Ingress has NO version hist  │
  │                                │     │ ❌ Ingress can't self-heal      │
  │ Risk: ZERO unmanaged resources │     │ ❌ DR: Ingress must be recreated│
  │                                │     │    manually (how? who knows?)   │
  └────────────────────────────────┘     │                                │
                                          │ Risk: ONE unmanaged resource =  │
                                          │ single point of failure         │
                                          └────────────────────────────────┘

  GOAL: 100% Git-managed. Every resource in production must exist in Git.
  For anihpj/jobpost: ZERO resources in the anihpj namespace that weren't
  created by ArgoCD from the anihpj-gitops repo.

If the Ingress was created manually via kubectl and NOT in Git, the system is only PARTIALLY GitOps-managed. That resource is a ticking time bomb — no version history, no audit trail, no self-healing. The goal: 100% Git-managed.

GitOps Terminology Q19 of 200
What is a "State Store" in GitOps?
A) The repository or storage system where the desired state is stored. Most commonly a Git repo (GitHub/GitLab), but can also be an S3 bucket, OCI registry, or any versioned storage. The State Store is the "source of truth"
B) A Kubernetes ConfigMap that stores the current state of all running pods
C) An in-memory cache inside the ArgoCD controller that holds reconciliation state
D) The etcd database inside the Kubernetes control plane
✅ Correct Answer: A

While Git is the most common state store, the GitOps principles don't mandate Git specifically. Flux CD supports OCIRepository (OCI registries) and Bucket (S3/GCS) as state stores. The key requirement: it must be versioned and immutable.

📝 Detailed Explanation — State Store Options
State Store Comparison — Git vs OCI vs S3
  STATE STORE    VERSIONED?  IMMUTABLE?  REVIEWABLE?  AUDITABLE?   BEST FOR
  ────────────   ──────────  ──────────  ───────────  ──────────   ────────
  Git (GitHub)   ✅ SHA      ✅ Commits   ✅ PRs       ✅ git log   Standard
  OCI Registry   ✅ Digest   ✅ Manifests ❌ No PR     ❌ Limited   Air-gapped
  S3 (versioned) ✅ ObjectID ✅ Versions  ❌ No PR     ❌ Limited   No Git access
  ────────────   ──────────  ──────────  ───────────  ──────────   ────────

  The name "GitOps" reflects Git's dominance — but the PRINCIPLES
  work with any versioned, immutable state store.
🔬 Flux CD Multi-Source Architecture
Flux Sources — GitRepository, OCIRepository, Bucket
  FLUX SOURCE TYPES:
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GitRepository          OCIRepository           Bucket           │
  │  ┌──────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐  │
  │  │ GitHub/GitLab │      │ OCI Registry │      │ S3 / GCS     │  │
  │  │ (HTTPS/SSH)   │      │ (ECR, Docker │      │ / Azure Blob │  │
  │  │               │      │  Hub, etc.)  │      │              │  │
  │  └──────┬───────┘      └──────┬───────┘      └──────┬───────┘  │
  │         │                     │                     │          │
  │         ▼                     ▼                     ▼          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │              source-controller (Flux)                    │  │
  │  │  Fetches from ANY source → Makes available to cluster    │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  For anihpj/jobpost: GitHub repo → Flux GitRepository source → reconciler.
  The deploy key is read-only — Flux can only READ from Git, never write.

Flux supports 3 source types. For anihpj/jobpost: the state store is a GitHub repository (anihpj-gitops), accessed via a read-only deploy key by ArgoCD running on the kubeadm cluster.

GitOps Terminology Q20 of 200
Why is Git the most common State Store for GitOps?
A) Git naturally provides all 5 required properties: (1) Versioned (commit SHA), (2) Immutable (history can't be rewritten), (3) Reviewable (Pull Requests), (4) Auditable (git log), (5) Distributed (every dev has full copy). No other storage system provides all 5 natively
B) Because the CNCF requires all GitOps tools to use Git specifically
C) Because Git is the only storage system that supports YAML files
D) Because ArgoCD and Flux only work with Git repositories
✅ Correct Answer: A

Git's design — cryptographic hashes, distributed architecture, merge workflows — aligns perfectly with GitOps requirements. The name "GitOps" reflects how central Git is, though the principles work with any versioned state store.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Git's 5 Built-In Superpowers
Why Git Wins — 5 Properties No Other System Has
  PROPERTY          GIT PROVIDES...                  WHY IT MATTERS
  ───────────────   ──────────────────────────────   ──────────────────────
  ① VERSIONED       Every commit = SHA hash          Rollback to any point
  ② IMMUTABLE       History can't change (w/o force) Trust — what was reviewed
                                                      IS what was deployed
  ③ REVIEWABLE      Pull Requests + code review      Peer validation before
                                                      changes reach production
  ④ AUDITABLE       git log --author --since         Compliance: who changed
                    git blame shows every line        what, when, and why
  ⑤ DISTRIBUTED     Every clone = full copy           No single point of
                                                      failure; work offline
  ───────────────   ──────────────────────────────   ──────────────────────

  S3 + versioning: ✅ ①②, ❌ ③④⑤ (no PRs, no git blame, no distributed)
  OCI registry:    ✅ ①②, ❌ ③④⑤ (no PRs, limited audit, no distributed)
  Git:             ✅①②③④⑤ (ALL FIVE — the complete package)
🔬 For anihpj/jobpost — Git's 5 Properties in Practice
How anihpj/jobpost Uses Each of Git's 5 Superpowers
  GIT PROPERTY         HOW ANIHPJ/JOBPOST USES IT
  ─────────────────    ─────────────────────────────────────────────
  ① VERSIONED          Every YAML change → commit SHA a1b2c3d
                        Rollback = git revert a1b2c3d → push

  ② IMMUTABLE          Branch protection blocks force-push
                        What was reviewed IS what runs in kubeadm

  ③ REVIEWABLE         PR required before merge to environments/prod/
                        Peer reviews catch: wrong image tag, missing TLS

  ④ AUDITABLE          git log -- environments/prod/ → who changed what
                        Compliance: prove config state at any point in time

  ⑤ DISTRIBUTED        Every developer has full clone of anihpj-gitops
                        Work offline, commit, push when connected
  ─────────────────    ─────────────────────────────────────────────

The anihpj-gitops Git repo on GitHub provides all 5 properties. Every YAML change is a commit with SHA, PRs require peer review, git log provides audit trail for compliance, and every developer has a local clone for offline work.

GitOps Terminology Q21 of 200
Can you use an S3 bucket as a State Store for GitOps?
A) YES — Flux CD supports Bucket sources (S3, GCS, Azure Blob). The bucket must store Kubernetes manifests and have object versioning enabled. Useful for air-gapped environments or when Git isn't available. However, S3 lacks PR reviews and collaboration features
B) No — GitOps strictly requires a Git repository; S3 is not supported by any GitOps tool
C) Only if the S3 bucket is configured with a Git-compatible API layer on top
D) Yes, but only for development environments — production requires Git
✅ Correct Answer: A

GitOps principles only require a "versioned and immutable" state store — not specifically Git. S3 with object versioning satisfies this. Flux CD's Bucket source makes this practical. However, S3 lacks PR reviews, CI integration, and Git's collaboration features.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Flux Bucket Source
S3 as State Store — How Flux Makes It Work
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FLUX BUCKET SOURCE (S3)                                        │
  │                                                                 │

  │    region: us-east-1                                            │
  │    interval: 3m                ← Poll S3 every 3 min            │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ CI writes │────▶│ S3 Bucket│────▶│ Flux source-controller   │ │
  │  │ manifests │     │(versioned)│    │ fetches + reconciles     │ │
  │  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  USE CASE: Air-gapped environment with no internet access.
  CI syncs manifests to an internal S3 bucket. Flux pulls from S3.
  No Git required. Still GitOps — just not "Git" Ops.

  Which state store should anihpj/jobpost use?
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │   ├─ YES → Use GitHub: anihpj-gitops repo ← STANDARD            │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  For anihpj/jobpost: GitHub is the standard choice. S3 is only
  for extreme air-gapped scenarios where even an internal Git
  server isn't feasible.

For anihpj/jobpost: The standard state store is GitHub (anihpj-gitops repo). S3 would only be considered if the kubeadm cluster operated in a fully air-gapped environment with no access to external Git services. Even then, a local GitLab/Gitea instance would typically be preferred over S3 due to the collaboration features.

GitOps Terminology Q22 of 200
In GitOps, where does the anihpj/jobpost desired state live?
A) In the Git repository (anihpj-gitops/environments/). Specifically: base/ (common), dev/ (DEBUG=True), staging/ (TLS, medium resources), prod/ (TLS, high resources, PDB). Each environment has its desired state in Git
B) In the Kubernetes cluster itself — the current running state IS the desired state
C) In the ArgoCD database — ArgoCD stores the desired state internally after the first sync
D) In the CI/CD pipeline configuration (Jenkinsfile, GitHub Actions YAML)
✅ Correct Answer: A

Each environment has its desired state in Git. The reconciler watches the appropriate path for each cluster and syncs accordingly. This is "environment-as-code" — dev, staging, and prod are just different directories in a Git repo.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Environment-as-Code
anihpj-gitops/environments/ — Desired State Per Environment
  anihpj-gitops/
  └── environments/
      ├── base/                    ← Kustomize base (shared by all)
      │   ├── kustomization.yaml
      │   ├── postgres-sts.yaml
      │   ├── django-api-deploy.yaml
      │   └── ...
      │
      ├── dev/                     ← DEV desired state
      │   ├── kustomization.yaml   ← Base + patches for dev
      │   ├── configmap-patch.yaml ← DEBUG=True, LOG_LEVEL=DEBUG
      │   └── ingress-patch.yaml   ← dev.jobpost.local (no TLS)
      │       ↓ ArgoCD watches this path → syncs to kind-cka-lab
      │
      ├── staging/                 ← STAGING desired state
      │   ├── kustomization.yaml   ← Base + patches for staging
      │   ├── configmap-patch.yaml ← DEBUG=False, LOG_LEVEL=INFO
      │   └── ingress-patch.yaml   ← staging.jobpost.com + TLS
      │       ↓ ArgoCD watches this path → syncs to kind-cl1
      │
      └── prod/                    ← PRODUCTION desired state
          ├── kustomization.yaml   ← Base + patches for prod
          ├── configmap-patch.yaml ← DEBUG=False, LOG_LEVEL=WARNING
          ├── ingress-patch.yaml   ← jobpost.com + TLS
          └── pdb.yaml             ← minAvailable: 2
              ↓ ArgoCD watches this path → syncs to kubeadm
🔬 One Repo, Three Environments, Three Clusters
Git → ArgoCD → Cluster Mapping for anihpj/jobpost
  GIT REPO (anihpj-gitops)          ARGOCD APPLICATION          TARGET CLUSTER
  ┌────────────────────────┐       ┌────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐
  │ environments/          │       │ app-anihpj-dev     │─────▶│ kind-cka-lab │
  │   dev/       ──────────┼──────▶│ (watches dev/)     │      │ (dev env)    │
  │                        │       └────────────────────┘      └──────────────┘
  │   staging/   ──────────┼──────▶┌────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐
  │                        │       │ app-anihpj-staging │─────▶│ kind-cl1     │
  │   prod/      ──────────┼──────▶│ (watches staging/) │      │ (staging)    │
  │                        │       └────────────────────┘      └──────────────┘
  │                        │       ┌────────────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐
  │                        │       │ app-anihpj-prod    │─────▶│ kubeadm      │
  │                        │       │ (watches prod/)    │      │ (production) │
  └────────────────────────┘       └────────────────────┘      └──────────────┘

  ONE Git repo. THREE paths. THREE ArgoCD Applications. THREE clusters.
  Changing environments/prod/ only affects kubeadm. Changing dev/ only
  affects kind-cka-lab. Git IS the single source of truth for ALL of them.

Each ArgoCD Application points to a different path in the same Git repo. For anihpj/jobpost: dev → kind-cka-lab, staging → kind-cl1, prod → kubeadm. One commit to environments/prod/ can change production — which is why PR reviews and branch protection are essential.

1.5 Feedback Loop & RollbackQ23-Q28

GitOps Terminology Q23 of 200
What is the "Feedback Loop" in GitOps?
A) The continuous cycle: Git (desired state) → Reconciler (syncs) → Cluster (runs) → Observability (monitors) → Feedback (alerts, dashboards) → Back to Git (improve). It's a closed loop — deploy → observe → learn → improve
B) A weekly email report summarizing all GitOps deployments across the organization
C) The process of developers giving feedback on each other's Pull Requests before merging
D) A monitoring dashboard that shows only CPU and memory usage of Kubernetes pods
✅ Correct Answer: A

GitOps is not just about deploying — it's about KNOWING if the deployment worked. The feedback loop closes the circle: deploy → observe → learn → improve (via new Git commits).

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Closed Loop
The GitOps Feedback Loop — Deploy → Observe → Learn → Improve
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     THE GITOPS FEEDBACK LOOP                         │
  │                                                                     │
  │  ① GIT (Desired State)                                              │
  │     │  Developer commits YAML change                                │
  │     ▼                                                               │
  │  ② RECONCILER (ArgoCD)                                              │
  │     │  Detects change → Syncs to cluster                            │
  │     ▼                                                               │
  │  ③ CLUSTER (kubeadm)                                                │
  │     │  New pods running, traffic flowing                             │
  │     ▼                                                               │
  │  ④ OBSERVABILITY (Prometheus + Grafana)                             │
  │     │  Metrics: error rate, latency, CPU, health probes             │
  │     ▼                                                               │
  │  ⑤ FEEDBACK (Alerts + Dashboards)                                   │
  │     │  Slack: "anihpj-prod: Synced ✅"                               │
  │     │  Grafana: error rate 0.1% (normal)                            │
  │     │  ArgoCD UI: Synced + Healthy ✅                                │
  │     ▼                                                               │
  │  ⑥ LEARN & IMPROVE                                                  │
  │     │  If error rate spikes → git revert + push                     │
  │     │  If healthy → continue monitoring                             │
  │     └───────▶ Back to ① (new Git commit with fix) ──────────────────│
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔬 Without the Feedback Loop — "Push and Pray"
Traditional CI/CD vs GitOps — Visibility Comparison
  TRADITIONAL CI/CD ("Push and Pray")     GITOPS (Closed Feedback Loop)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐         ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ CI: "Deploy successful!"   │         │ ArgoCD: "Synced + Healthy ✅"│
  │                            │         │ Grafana: "p99 = 200ms ✅"   │
  │ Developer: "Is it really?  │         │ Prometheus: "Error rate 0%" │
  │ Let me check..."            │         │ Slack: "Deploy complete ✅" │
  │                            │         │                            │
  │ • Checks logs manually     │         │ • All metrics in dashboard  │
  │ • Asks in Slack: "Is prod  │         │ • Git commit shows deployed │
  │   working?"                │         │   SHA in ArgoCD UI          │
  │ • 10 min later: "Seems OK" │         │ • Instant confidence        │
  │                            │         │                            │
  │ ❌ No automated feedback   │         │ ✅ Automated, continuous    │
  │ ❌ Relies on humans asking │         │ ✅ No "is it deployed?" Qs  │
  └────────────────────────────┘         └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: After ArgoCD syncs, Prometheus monitors error rates and latency. Grafana dashboards show real-time health. If the new deployment causes errors, alerts fire in Slack within seconds. The team can git revert immediately. The loop closes: deploy → observe → learn → fix.

GitOps Terminology Q24 of 200
How does ArgoCD provide feedback about deployment status?
A) ArgoCD shows: (1) Sync Status (Synced/OutOfSync), (2) Health Status (Healthy/Degraded), (3) Operation State, (4) Commit SHA + author, (5) Diff view. All visible in dashboard and CLI (argocd app get)
B) ArgoCD sends a daily PDF report via email to all team members
C) ArgoCD only shows a green or red light — no detailed information is available
D) ArgoCD relies entirely on external monitoring tools like Datadog for deployment status
✅ Correct Answer: A

This visibility is a massive improvement over traditional CI/CD where you "push and pray." GitOps gives you immediate visual confirmation of what's deployed, who deployed it, and whether it's healthy — all in one dashboard.

CLI: argocd app get anihpj-prod — shows sync status, health, revision, and resource tree. UI: Dashboard with color-coded status (green=Synced+Healthy, yellow=OutOfSync, red=Degraded) plus network graph showing all resources. For anihpj/jobpost: the ArgoCD dashboard on the kubeadm cluster provides instant visibility into every deployment.

GitOps Terminology Q25 of 200
What is a "Rollback" in GitOps?
A) A rollback is NOT a separate button — it's a Git REVERT. You revert the problematic commit, push to Git, and the reconciler syncs back to the previous good state. Rollback = git revert + git push. Simple, fast, auditable
B) A rollback requires running a special "argocd rollback" CLI command that triggers a database restore
C) A rollback is when the reconciler automatically deletes all resources and recreates them from scratch
D) A rollback requires opening a support ticket with the cloud provider to restore a cluster snapshot
✅ Correct Answer: A

In traditional CI/CD, rollback is a special procedure. In GitOps, rollback is just Git — revert the commit, push, done. Anyone with Git access can roll back. It leaves an audit trail and is reproducible.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Rollback = Git Revert
GitOps Rollback — 3 Steps, Fully Auditable
  STEP 1: IDENTIFY THE BAD COMMIT          STEP 2: REVERT
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ git log --oneline -5       │          │ git revert a1b2c3d         │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ a1b2c3d Update API to v1.2.5 ← BAD │  │ Creates NEW commit that    │
  │ e4f5g6h Update API to v1.2.4        │  │ UNDOES a1b2c3d            │
  │ i7j8k9l Add HPA config              │  │                            │
  │                            │          │ Commit message auto-gen:    │
  │ The BAD commit is a1b2c3d  │          │ "Revert 'Update API v1.2.5'"│
  └────────────────────────────┘          └───────────┬────────────────┘
                                                      │
  STEP 3: PUSH & SYNC                                  ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ git push origin main                                           │
  │                                                                │
  │ ArgoCD detects new commit (revert) → Syncs cluster to v1.2.4  │
  │ Status: Synced + Healthy ✅                                     │
  │                                                                │
  │ Git history now shows:                                         │
  │   x7y8z9w Revert "Update API to v1.2.5"  ← Alice (1 min ago) │
  │   a1b2c3d Update API to v1.2.5            ← Alice (5 min ago) │
  │   e4f5g6h Update API to v1.2.4            ← Alice (1 hour ago) │
  │                                                                │
  │ ✅ Complete audit trail of what happened and the recovery     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔬 ArgoCD UI Rollback vs Git Revert

ArgoCD UI also has a "History and Rollback" button — click a previous revision → "Rollback." This is FASTER but creates drift (cluster ≠ Git HEAD). The GitOps way: always git revert so Git remains the source of truth. For anihpj/jobpost: if v1.2.5 breaks the job board, Alice runs git revert a1b2c3d && git push — ArgoCD syncs back to v1.2.4 within 3 minutes.

GitOps Terminology Q26 of 200
If anihpj-api v1.2.4 has a bug, how do you roll back with GitOps?
A) Option 1: git revert the commit that changed the image tag → git push → ArgoCD syncs back to v1.2.3. Option 2: ArgoCD UI → "History and Rollback" → select v1.2.3 → Rollback. The Git revert is preferred (keeps Git as source of truth)
B) SSH into the kubeadm cluster and run: kubectl rollout undo deploy anihpj-api -n anihpj
C) Delete the anihpj-api Deployment and recreate it from a backup YAML file stored on the DevOps engineer's laptop
D) Wait for ArgoCD to automatically detect the bug and roll back — it has built-in bug detection
✅ Correct Answer: A

Both methods work, but the Git revert is the GitOps way — it ensures Git always reflects what SHOULD be running. The ArgoCD UI rollback is convenient but creates drift between Git HEAD and the cluster until you also revert in Git.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Two Paths, One Preferred
Rollback Path Comparison — Git Revert vs ArgoCD UI
  GIT REVERT (Preferred)                   ARGOCD UI ROLLBACK (Fast)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ ① git revert a1b2c3d      │          │ ① ArgoCD UI → App → History│
  │ ② git push origin main    │          │ ② Select revision e4f5g6h  │
  │ ③ ArgoCD detects new HEAD │          │ ③ Click "Rollback"         │
  │ ④ Syncs cluster to revert │          │ ④ Cluster → v1.2.3         │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ Git HEAD: v1.2.3 ✅        │          │ Git HEAD: v1.2.4 ❌         │
  │ Cluster: v1.2.3 ✅         │          │ Cluster: v1.2.3 ✅          │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ ✅ Git = truth              │          │ ⚠️ DRIFT! Fix: git revert   │
  │ ✅ Full audit trail        │          │    after incident to sync  │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: During an incident, use the ArgoCD UI rollback for SPEED (instant). Then immediately git revert to align Git with the cluster. Never leave Git out of sync with production.

GitOps Terminology Q27 of 200
What is the difference between ArgoCD's "Sync" and "Rollback" operations?
A) Sync: Move cluster TO the latest desired state in Git (HEAD). Rollback: Move cluster TO a PREVIOUS desired state by syncing to an older Git commit. Both are sync operations — just targeting different Git revisions
B) Sync creates resources; Rollback only deletes resources
C) Sync is automated; Rollback is always manual
D) Sync works on any cluster; Rollback only works on production clusters
✅ Correct Answer: A

There's no special "rollback" mechanism — only sync. The reconciler doesn't distinguish "deployment" from "rollback." It just makes the cluster match the specified Git revision. This elegant simplicity is a core GitOps benefit.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Sync Is Sync, Regardless of Direction
Sync Forward vs Sync Backward — Same Operation
  SYNC FORWARD (Deploy)                    SYNC BACKWARD (Rollback)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Git HEAD: v1.2.4 (new)    │          │ Git TARGET: v1.2.3 (old)   │
  │ Cluster: v1.2.3            │          │ Cluster: v1.2.4             │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ ArgoCD: "Make cluster      │          │ ArgoCD: "Make cluster      │
  │ match Git HEAD"            │          │ match this older revision" │
  │                            │          │                            │
  │ Action: UPDATE image       │          │ Action: DOWNGRADE image    │
  │ Result: v1.2.4 running     │          │ Result: v1.2.3 running     │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘

  SAME MECHANISM: Compare desired revision ≠ cluster → Apply diff.
  ArgoCD doesn't know or care if you're going forward or backward.

For anihpj/jobpost: Whether deploying v1.2.4 or rolling back to v1.2.3, ArgoCD does the exact same thing — sync the cluster to match the target Git revision.

GitOps Terminology Q28 of 200
What feedback does Git itself provide in a GitOps workflow?
A) (1) Commit status (CI checks ✅/❌), (2) PR reviews (peer approval), (3) Git history (who/what/when/why), (4) Tags (release versions), (5) Branch protection (prevents unreviewed changes). Git IS the approval system
B) Git only stores code — it provides no feedback mechanism of its own
C) Git sends push notifications to the DevOps team's mobile phones on every commit
D) Git automatically runs load tests on every merged Pull Request
✅ Correct Answer: A

Git's built-in features become your deployment pipeline's control plane. You don't need a separate "deployment approval system" — Git IS your approval system (PR review + branch protection = deployment gate).

📝 Detailed Explanation — Git as the Control Plane
Git's 5 Feedback Mechanisms in GitOps
  GIT FEATURE           HOW IT FEEDS BACK INTO GITOPS
  ──────────────────    ─────────────────────────────────────────────
  ① Commit Status       CI runs on every commit → ✅/❌ shown on PR
                         ❌ = merge BLOCKED → prevents bad configs
  ② PR Reviews          Peers review YAML changes before merge
                         "Did you mean to change replicas to 1?"
  ③ Git History         git log → who changed what, when, why
                         git blame → every line attributed to author
  ④ Tags                v1.2.4 tag = this exact state is a release
                         Rollback = checkout v1.2.3 tag
  ⑤ Branch Protection   Require PR + passing CI before merge to prod
                         No force-push → history is immutable
  ──────────────────    ─────────────────────────────────────────────

For anihpj/jobpost: GitHub branch protection on the prod branch requires PR review + passing CI checks. No one can change production configs without peer approval. This is your deployment approval gate — implemented entirely in Git.

1.6 Terminology in Practice — anihpj/jobpost WalkthroughQ29-Q34

GitOps Terminology Q29 of 200
Walk through a complete GitOps flow for updating anihpj-api from v1.2.3 → v1.2.4. Match each step to the correct term.
A) Step 1: Dev changes code → CI builds v1.2.4 → pushes to ECR. Step 2: CI updates image tag in Git. Step 3: CI commits+pushes (State Store). Step 4: ArgoCD detects commit (Reconciler). Step 5: 3-way diff detects drift. Step 6: ArgoCD syncs → Desired State reached. Step 7: Prometheus confirms health → Feedback Loop closes
B) All 7 steps are performed manually by the DevOps engineer using kubectl commands
C) Steps 1-3 don't exist — the developer directly edits YAML in the ArgoCD dashboard
D) Steps 4-7 are skipped — the CI pipeline deploys directly to the cluster without a reconciler
✅ Correct Answer: A

Every GitOps term maps to a concrete step in this workflow. This is GitOps in action — not just theory.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Full 7-Step Flow
anihpj-api v1.2.3 → v1.2.4 — Complete GitOps Deployment
  STEP 1: DEV + CI                          STEP 4-7: GITOPS CD
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Developer edits app code   │           │ ④ ARGOCD DETECTS (poll/webhook)
  │ git push → GitHub Actions  │           │    "New commit a1b2c3d in Git"
  │                            │           │                            │
  │ CI: Build Docker image     │           │ ⑤ 3-WAY DIFF                │
  │ CI: Trivy scan (no CRIT)   │           │    Git: v1.2.4              │
  │ CI: Push to ECR            │           │    Cluster: v1.2.3          │
  │                            │           │    → DRIFT DETECTED         │
  │ CI: Clone anihpj-gitops    │           │                            │
  │ CI: Update image tag in    │           │ ⑥ ARGOCD SYNCS              │
  │   staging/django-api.yaml  │           │    RollingUpdate: new pods  │
  │ CI: git commit + git push  │           │    Old pods terminated      │
  └───────────┬────────────────┘           │                            │
              │                            │ ⑦ FEEDBACK LOOP             │
  STEP 2-3: STATE STORE                    │    ArgoCD: Synced ✅        │
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           │    Prometheus: 200ms p99 ✅ │
  │ Git repo updated with      │           │    Slack: "Deployed ✅"     │
  │ new image tag reference    │           └────────────────────────────┘
  │ Commit: a1b2c3d            │
  │ Author: CI Bot             │           ⏱️ TOTAL TIME: ~5-7 min (CI build + GitOps sync)
  └────────────────────────────┘           (CI: ~4 min build, GitOps: ~1-3 min sync)

For anihpj/jobpost: This exact flow runs every time code changes. The CI pipeline builds, scans, and pushes the image, then updates Git. ArgoCD takes over from there — pulling the change and syncing to the kubeadm cluster. CI stops at Git. CD starts at Git. Git is the handoff point.

GitOps Terminology Q30 of 200
What is the "Single Source of Truth" in the anihpj/jobpost GitOps workflow?
A) The Git repository (anihpj-gitops). It contains ALL configs for ALL environments. No wiki, no shared drive, no "ask Dave." Git is THE truth. If it's not in Git, it doesn't exist in the cluster
B) The ArgoCD dashboard — whatever it shows as the current state is the truth
C) The Kubernetes cluster itself — the running state IS the source of truth
D) The CI/CD pipeline configuration — Jenkinsfile or GitHub Actions workflow
✅ Correct Answer: A

Single Source of Truth = no ambiguity. When an incident happens at 3 AM, the on-call engineer looks at Git to know EXACTLY what should be running. No guessing. No "I think Bob changed something last week."

📝 Detailed Explanation
Before GitOps vs After — Finding the Truth at 3 AM
  BEFORE GITOPS (3 AM Incident)              AFTER GITOPS (3 AM Incident)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 😰 On-call: "Why is prod   │           │ ✅ On-call: opens GitHub    │
  │    broken?"                 │           │    → anihpj-gitops repo    │
  │                             │           │    → environments/prod/    │
  │ • Checks cluster: 5 pods?  │           │                            │
  │ • "Bob said 3 last week..." │           │ • deploy-api.yaml:         │
  │ • Wiki says 2 (outdated)   │           │   replicas: 3              │
  │ • Slack: "Anyone know?"    │           │   image: v1.2.4            │
  │                             │           │                            │
  │ ⏱️ 20 minutes later:        │           │ • Cluster has 5 pods?       │
  │   "OK we think it should   │           │   → DRIFT! Git says 3.     │
  │    be 3 replicas..."        │           │   → Someone scaled manually│
  │                             │           │                            │
  │ ❌ Multiple "truths"        │           │ ⏱️ 30 seconds: Found the    │
  └────────────────────────────┘           │   truth. Git = 3 replicas. │
                                           │   ArgoCD will fix it.      │
                                           │                            │
                                           │ ✅ ONE truth: Git           │
                                           └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-gitops repo on GitHub IS the single source of truth — for dev, staging, and production. There is no other source.

GitOps Terminology Q31 of 200
What happens when you manually run kubectl delete deploy anihpj-api -n anihpj?
A) The Deployment is deleted, pods terminate, API goes DOWN. But within 3 minutes, ArgoCD detects: Cluster (no Deployment) ≠ Git (Deployment exists) → ArgoCD RECREATES the Deployment → pods start → API is back. This is Self-Healing / Drift Correction
B) Nothing happens — kubectl commands are blocked when GitOps is enabled
C) ArgoCD permanently deletes the Deployment YAML from Git to match the cluster state
D) The cluster sends an alert and waits 24 hours before recreating the Deployment
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is GitOps's "immune system" — the cluster fights back against manual changes. The reconciler detects the missing Deployment and recreates it from Git. Total downtime: ~3-5 minutes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Self-Healing Timeline — Delete → Detect → Recreate
  T0: kubectl delete deploy anihpj-api    STATUS: 💥 API DOWN
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  T1: ArgoCD polls (within 3 min)         STATUS: OutOfSync ❌
      Git: Deployment exists              Discovery: Cluster has NO Deployment
      Cluster: Deployment MISSING!        3-way diff → DRIFT DETECTED
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  T2: ArgoCD auto-syncs                   STATUS: Progressing
      Recreates Deployment from Git       Action: kubectl apply (via API)
      New pods start spinning up
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  T3: Pods Running + Ready                STATUS: Synced + Healthy ✅
      API is back online                  Recovery: ~4 min total downtime
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  WITHOUT GITOPS: API stays DOWN until a human notices and fixes it.

For anihpj/jobpost: Even if someone accidentally deletes the entire anihpj-api Deployment, ArgoCD brings it back within minutes. The kubeadm cluster is self-healing because the desired state lives in Git — and Git can't be deleted by kubectl.

A) Namespace + Secrets + ConfigMaps + StatefulSet(PostgreSQL) + Service(db) + Deployment(Redis) + Service(redis) + Deployment(api) + Service(api) + Deployment(web) + Service(web) + Ingress + NetworkPolicy + HPA + RBAC + PDB + ServiceAccount — ALL Git-managed
B) Just the anihpj-api Deployment — everything else is infrastructure, not part of the system
C) Only the resources visible in the ArgoCD dashboard — hidden resources don't count
D) The Docker images in the container registry — Kubernetes resources are not part of the managed system
✅ Correct Answer: A

17 resources, ALL in Git, ALL reconciled by ArgoCD. If any piece is managed outside Git, the system is only partially GitOps-managed. Goal: 100% Git-managed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — 17 Resources, 100% Git-Managed
  #  RESOURCE                  FILE IN environments/prod/
  ──  ───────────────────────  ─────────────────────────────
  1   Namespace                namespace.yaml
  2   Secret (DB password)     sealed-secrets.yaml

For anihpj/jobpost: Every one of these 17 resources is defined in Git and reconciled by ArgoCD to the kubeadm cluster. Nothing exists in the anihpj namespace that wasn't created by ArgoCD from Git.

GitOps Terminology Q33 of 200
Why does GitOps use the term "Continuous" instead of "Automated"?
A) "Automated" = one-time trigger (CI builds, then pushes, then STOPS). "Continuous" = process NEVER STOPS — reconciler keeps watching, comparing, correcting FOREVER. Automation is event-driven. Continuous is always-on. This is fundamental to GitOps
B) The CNCF trademarked "Continuous" and requires all member projects to use it
C) There is no difference — the terms are used interchangeably in GitOps documentation
D) "Automated" was already taken by the CI/CD industry, so GitOps chose "Continuous" to avoid confusion
✅ Correct Answer: A

CI/CD pipelines are automated (triggered by git push, end after deploy). GitOps reconciliation is continuous (runs forever). This is why GitOps provides self-healing — the reconciler keeps checking even when nothing changed in Git.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Automated vs Continuous — The Critical Distinction
  AUTOMATED (CI/CD Pipeline)                CONTINUOUS (GitOps Reconciler)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐           ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Trigger: git push          │           │ Trigger: Timer (every 3 min)│
  │ Action: Build → Test →     │           │   OR webhook event         │
  │   Deploy → DONE            │           │                            │
  │                            │           │ Action: Poll Git → Diff →  │
  │ After deploy:              │           │   Apply → Report → LOOP 🔄 │
  │   CI exits                 │           │                            │
  │   Pipeline FINISHED        │           │ After sync:                │
  │                            │           │   Wait 3 min → Poll again  │
  │ ❌ No drift detection      │           │   NEVER exits              │
  │ ❌ No self-healing         │           │                            │
  │ ❌ "Deploy and hope"       │           │ ✅ Self-healing active      │
  └────────────────────────────┘           │ ✅ Drift detection always on│
                                           └────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: ArgoCD never stops watching the kubeadm cluster. It's always on, always checking, always ready to correct drift. CI deploys once and stops. ArgoCD reconciles continuously forever.

GitOps Terminology Q34 of 200
What does "Rollback" mean when anihpj-api v1.2.4 causes database migration errors?
A) (1) git revert the image tag change. (2) git push. (3) ArgoCD syncs app back to v1.2.3. BUT: the DB migration may have ALREADY RUN. Rolling back the APP does NOT roll back the DATABASE. You need a separate DB rollback (Django migrate --reverse)
B) ArgoCD automatically reverses the database migration as part of the rollback process
C) Both the application and database are rolled back together by reverting the Git commit
D) Database migrations are not a concern in GitOps because Kubernetes handles them automatically
✅ Correct Answer: A

GitOps manages infrastructure and config — not data. Database migrations are application-level operations. Rolling back the app in Git does NOT undo the migration that already ran. Data is NOT in Git.

📝 Detailed Explanation
App Rollback ≠ Database Rollback — The Separation
  GITOPS ROLLBACK (App)                    DATABASE ROLLBACK (Separate)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐          ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ git revert (image tag)     │          │ python manage.py migrate   │
  │ git push                   │          │   anihpj 0004_reverse      │
  │ ArgoCD syncs → v1.2.3      │          │                            │
  │                            │          │ OR restore from backup:    │
  │ ✅ App is back to v1.2.3   │          │ pg_restore -d jobpost      │
  │                            │          │   backup_20260612.sql      │
  │ ⚠️ But DB schema is from   │          │                            │
  │   v1.2.4 migration!        │          │ ✅ DB schema restored      │
  └────────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────────────┘

  GITOPS MANAGES: YAML, Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps...
  GITOPS DOES NOT MANAGE: Database schemas, user data, file uploads...

For anihpj/jobpost: If v1.2.4 added a migration that broke the schema, git revert rolls back the app but the migration already ran. You need Django's migrate --reverse AND the git revert. GitOps handles the app. You handle the data.

1.7 Terminology Deep Dive & Edge CasesQ35-Q40

GitOps Terminology Q35 of 200
You notice anihpj-web is serving HTTP instead of HTTPS. Git shows the Ingress YAML has TLS configured. What term describes this?
A) State Drift — the cluster's actual state (no TLS) doesn't match Git's desired state (TLS configured). Someone may have edited the Ingress manually, the TLS cert expired, or cert-manager changed it. The reconciler will detect this in the next sync cycle
B) Configuration Error — the YAML in Git is wrong and needs to be fixed
C) Network Failure — the TLS certificate server is temporarily unreachable
D) Health Degradation — the application is unhealthy and ArgoCD will mark it Degraded
✅ Correct Answer: A

Drift detection catches configuration inconsistencies that would be hard to spot manually. The reconciler is your configuration auditor — it compares everything, not just what you remember to check.

📝 Detailed Explanation
HTTP vs HTTPS Drift — How It Happens
  GIT (Desired State)                CLUSTER (Actual State)          DRIFT?
  ─────────────────────────────────  ─────────────────────────────  ─────
  Ingress YAML:
    tls:                             Ingress live state:
    - hosts:                         No TLS section present          ❌ DRIFT!
      - jobpost.com                  Port 80 only (HTTP)
      secretName: jobpost-tls
                                     Possible causes:
  annotations:                       • Someone kubectl-edited Ingress
    cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer:  • TLS certificate expired
      letsencrypt-prod               • cert-manager modified Ingress
                                     • ClusterIssuer misconfigured

  ArgoCD will detect: Git says TLS ON, Cluster says TLS OFF → DRIFT.
  Auto-sync will restore TLS configuration from Git.

For anihpj/jobpost: If the job board suddenly serves HTTP instead of HTTPS, check Git first. If Git says TLS should be on, it's drift — ArgoCD will fix it. If Git also says TLS off, someone changed Git — check git log.

GitOps Terminology Q36 of 200
What is the Feedback Loop output in ArgoCD for anihpj/jobpost?
A) ArgoCD UI shows: (1) Application: Synced+Healthy ✅, (2) Current Revision: commit a1b2c3d, (3) Last Sync: 2 min ago, Duration: 45s, (4) Resources: 15/15 healthy, (5) Network graph: all resources visible
B) ArgoCD only outputs a simple "success" or "failure" message with no additional details
C) ArgoCD sends a CSV file with deployment metrics to the DevOps team's email
D) ArgoCD does not provide feedback — that's the job of external monitoring tools
✅ Correct Answer: A

The feedback loop gives you complete deployment visibility. You know WHO deployed WHAT, WHEN, and whether it's HEALTHY — all in one dashboard. No more "is the deploy done yet?" Slack messages.

What You See After a Successful anihpj/jobpost Deployment
  ARGOCD DASHBOARD — anihpj-prod
