  SCENARIO: CI pushed manifest with broken image, THEN tests failed
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ WRONG ORDER (what happened):                                   │
  │ 1. CI: git push (update image to v1.2.4)                      │
  │ 2. CI: run integration tests → FAIL!                           │
  │ 3. ArgoCD already synced v1.2.4 → PROD BROKEN                 │
  │                                                                │
  │ FIX:                                                           │
  │ 1. git revert HEAD → push                                     │
  │ 2. ArgoCD syncs revert → back to v1.2.3 → PROD RECOVERED      │
  │ 3. Fix the pipeline: UPDATE GIT AFTER TESTS PASS!              │
  │                                                                │
  │ PREVENTION (correct order):                                    │
  │ 1. CI: run ALL tests FIRST                                     │
  │ 2. CI: ONLY IF all pass → git push (update manifest)           │
  │ 3. ArgoCD syncs tested image                                   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  Golden rule: NEVER update Git BEFORE tests pass.

For anihpj/jobpost: The CI pipeline must run ALL tests BEFORE updating the Git manifest. If a broken manifest somehow reaches Git, `git revert` + push triggers ArgoCD to sync back to the previous known-good state. But prevention is better — tests first, Git update second.

Related Practices Q130 of 200
Explain the "Git as middleware" pattern in detail.
A) Git serves as the COMMUNICATION BUS between CI and CD. CI producers write deployment intent (new image tags, config changes) to Git. CD consumers (reconcilers) read from Git and apply to clusters. Git provides: (1) Reliable messaging (commits are atomic), (2) Ordering (commit history = event order), (3) Durability (commits persist), (4) Audit (who/what/when). Git is the event log — CI and CD are decoupled producers/consumers
B) Git middleware means Git replaces the CI server entirely
C) Git middleware only works with GitHub — GitLab and Bitbucket are incompatible with the pattern
D) Git as middleware means you need a separate message queue (Kafka) alongside Git
✅ Correct Answer: A
Git as Middleware — The CI/CD Event Bus
  PRODUCERS (CI)                            CONSUMERS (CD)
  ┌──────────┐                              ┌──────────┐
  │ GitHub   │──── git push ────┐           │ ArgoCD   │─── sync → Cluster A
  │ Actions  │                  │           └──────────┘
  └──────────┘                  │           ┌──────────┐
  ┌──────────┐                  ▼           │ ArgoCD   │─── sync → Cluster B
  │ Jenkins  │──── git push ──▶ GIT ◀────── │ (hub)    │
  └──────────┘                  ▲           └──────────┘
  ┌──────────┐                  │           ┌──────────┐
  │ GitLab   │──── git push ────┘           │ Flux CD  │─── sync → Cluster C
  │ CI       │                              └──────────┘
  └──────────┘
  Git = the EVENT LOG. CI = producers (write). CD = consumers (read).
  Atomic commits = reliable messaging. Commit order = event ordering.
  History = durable audit log. SHA = cryptographic proof.

Git acts as the communication bus between CI and CD. Producers write deployment intent. Consumers read and apply. Atomic commits = reliable messaging. History = durable audit log.

🔬 Git as Middleware — Producer-Consumer Architecture
Event-Driven Architecture — Git as the Event Log
  PRODUCERS (write events):                  CONSUMERS (read events):
  ┌────────────────────┐                    ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ GitHub Actions (CI)│──git push──┐       │ ArgoCD (kubeadm)           │
  │ Jenkins (legacy CI) │──git push──┤       │ ArgoCD (kind-cl1)          │
  │ Flux Image Auto     │──git push──┼──GIT──│ ArgoCD (kind-cl2)          │
  │ Developer (human)   │──git push──┘       │ Flux (kind-cka DR)         │
  └────────────────────┘                    └────────────────────────────┘
  GIT PROPERTIES AS MIDDLEWARE:
  ✅ Atomic: Commit succeeds or fails completely
  ✅ Ordered: Commit history = event ordering
  ✅ Durable: Commits persist forever (immutable)
  ✅ Replayable: Any consumer can replay from any point
  ✅ Decoupled: Producers and consumers don't know each other

For anihpj/jobpost: Multiple producers (GitHub Actions, Jenkins, Flux Image Automation, human developers) write to Git. Multiple consumers (ArgoCD on 3 clusters, Flux on DR) read from Git. They don't know about each other. Git is the decoupled event bus — enterprise integration pattern, GitOps style.

Related Practices Q131 of 200
How do Push vs Pull CD metrics compare (deployment frequency, lead time, MTTR)?
A) Push CD: CI tool pushes to cluster (kubectl apply from Jenkins). Pull CD (GitOps): reconciler pulls from Git. Metrics comparison: Deployment Frequency — Pull enables higher frequency (no CI cluster access bottleneck). Lead Time — Pull is slightly slower (3-min poll interval vs instant push). MTTR — Pull is FASTER (git revert + auto-sync vs manual kubectl rollback). Change Failure Rate — Pull is LOWER (PR gate + immutability reduces bad deploys)
B) Push CD is always faster and more reliable than Pull CD (GitOps)
C) GitOps Pull CD has worse MTTR because you have to wait for the reconciler
D) DORA metrics don't apply to GitOps — they're only for traditional CI/CD
✅ Correct Answer: A
DORA Metrics: Push CD vs GitOps Pull CD
  DORA METRIC            PUSH CD (CI→Cluster)     PULL CD (GitOps)
  ─────────────────────  ───────────────────────  ───────────────────────
  Deployment Frequency   Medium (CI bottleneck)   HIGH (no CI dep)
  Lead Time (commit→prod) FASTER (instant push)   Slightly slower (3-min)
  MTTR (recovery time)   Slower (manual rollback) FASTER (git revert+sync)
  Change Failure Rate    Higher (less gate)        LOWER (PR + immutable)
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Pull CD trades ~3 min of lead time for LOWER failure rate and FASTER MTTR.
  For most orgs, this is a NET POSITIVE trade. Webhooks reduce lead time gap.

Pull CD trades ~3 min of lead time for lower failure rate and faster MTTR. For most organizations, this is a net positive. Webhooks reduce the lead time gap.

🔬 DORA Metrics — anihpj Before vs After GitOps
anihpj DORA Metrics — Push CD vs GitOps Pull CD
  METRIC                   │ PUSH CD (before)  │ PULL CD (GitOps)  │ CHANGE
  ─────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────────────────┼───────
  Deployment Frequency     │ 2/week             │ 10/day            │ +35x
  Lead Time (commit→prod)  │ 4 hours            │ 8 min (webhook)   │ -30x
  MTTR (recovery)          │ 45 min (manual)    │ 3 min (git revert)│ -15x
  Change Failure Rate      │ 15%                │ 3%                │ -5x
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Pull CD (GitOps) improves ALL 4 DORA metrics simultaneously.
  Trade-off: ~3 min poll delay. Worth it for the other improvements.

For anihpj/jobpost: After adopting GitOps, deployment frequency increased 35x (from 2/week to 10/day), lead time dropped 30x (from 4 hours to 8 minutes), MTTR dropped 15x (from 45 min manual to 3 min git revert), and change failure rate dropped 5x (from 15% to 3%). All four DORA metrics improved.

Related Practices Q132 of 200
How to integrate GitOps CD with legacy CI systems?
A) Legacy CI (Jenkins, Bamboo, TeamCity) can integrate with GitOps by: (1) CI's final step: update Git manifest + push (same as modern CI). (2) Use a "GitOps bridge" script: CI deploys via kubectl for now, but ALSO writes to Git for future GitOps migration. (3) Phased migration: start with dev environment on GitOps, keep prod on legacy CI, migrate one env at a time. (4) Adapter pattern: legacy CI triggers ArgoCD sync via API (argocd app sync) instead of kubectl. Git is the universal adapter
B) Legacy CI systems cannot work with GitOps — they must be replaced first
C) Legacy CI works with GitOps only if using GitHub Actions as a bridge
D) You need a special GitOps plugin for Jenkins to make it work
✅ Correct Answer: A
Legacy CI → GitOps Migration Path
  PHASE 1 (Today):                          PHASE 2 (Transition):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Jenkins builds → tests     │            │ Jenkins builds → tests     │
  │ Jenkins kubectl apply      │            │ Jenkins writes to Git      │
  │ (push to cluster)          │            │ ArgoCD syncs dev (GitOps)  │
  │                            │            │ Jenkins kubectl to prod    │
  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘
  PHASE 3 (All GitOps):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Jenkins builds → tests     │  Git is the universal adapter.
  │ Jenkins writes to Git      │  Any CI system that can git push
  │ ArgoCD syncs ALL envs      │  can integrate with GitOps.
  └────────────────────────────┘

Any CI system that can git push can integrate with GitOps. Git is the universal adapter. Phase migration: start with one env, prove value, expand.

🔬 Legacy CI Migration — Jenkins to GitOps for anihpj
Phase Migration — Jenkins → Jenkins + Git → Full GitOps
  PHASE 1: Jenkins deploys via kubectl (current state)
  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Jenkins → kubectl apply → Cluster                             │
  └────────────────────────────┘
  PHASE 2: Jenkins writes to Git, ArgoCD syncs dev only
  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Jenkins → git push → ArgoCD (dev) → dev cluster               │
  │ Jenkins → kubectl apply (staging + prod)                      │
  └────────────────────────────┘
  PHASE 3: Full GitOps — Jenkins only writes to Git
  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Jenkins → git push → ArgoCD syncs ALL environments            │
  │ Jenkins has NO cluster access                                  │
  └────────────────────────────┘
  For anihpj: Phase 2 — GitOps for dev/staging, legacy for prod.
  Migrate prod last, after proving GitOps reliability on lower envs.

For anihpj/jobpost: If migrating from Jenkins, start with Phase 2 — Jenkins writes to Git for dev/staging (GitOps), but still kubectl-applies to production (legacy). After proving GitOps reliability on lower environments, migrate production last. Never migrate prod first.

🏗️ Category 4: GitOps Patterns (20%)Q133–Q172

"This category covers the PATTERNS and ARCHITECTURES of GitOps — how you structure repos, manage multiple environments, handle progressive delivery, and choose between pull vs event-driven reconciliation. These are the decisions you make when IMPLEMENTING GitOps in production."

4.1 Deployment & Release PatternsQ133–Q142

GitOps Patterns Q133 of 200
What is the "App of Apps" pattern in ArgoCD?
A) A ROOT ArgoCD Application that manages OTHER ArgoCD Applications (its children). The root app is defined in one Git repo. Each child app points to a different Git repo/path/environment. When you sync the root app, ArgoCD creates and manages ALL child apps — bootstrapping your entire GitOps setup from ONE manifest. Benefits: single bootstrap command, declarative management of Applications themselves, easy to add/remove environments
B) An ArgoCD feature that deploys multiple container images in a single Application
C) A Helm chart that contains multiple sub-charts for different microservices
D) A pattern where one Kubernetes namespace contains all applications
✅ Correct Answer: A
App of Apps = GitOps Inception
  ROOT APP (argocd/app-of-apps.yaml)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1                               │
  │ kind: Application                                              │
  │ spec:                                                          │
  │   source: repoURL: anihpj-gitops, path: argocd/                │
  │   destination: server: https://kubernetes.default.svc          │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
          │ CREATES 3 CHILD APPS
          ▼
  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
  │ anihpj-dev   │  │ anihpj-stag  │  │ anihpj-prod  │
  │ (dev/ env)   │  │ (staging/)   │  │ (prod/)      │
  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘
  Sync the ROOT once → all children created → each syncs its manifests.
  Adding a 4th env = just one more entry in the root YAML.

App of Apps = GitOps inception. One root Application manages all child Applications. Benefits: single bootstrap command, declarative management of Applications themselves.

🔬 anihpj App of Apps — Production Bootstrap
anihpj Root App — The Master Switch for All Environments
  argocd/app-of-apps.yaml (ROOT — synced ONCE):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ applications:                                                  │
  │   - name: anihpj-dev       path: environments/dev/             │
  │     syncPolicy: automated, prune=true, selfHeal=true           │
  │   - name: anihpj-staging   path: environments/staging/         │
  │     syncPolicy: automated, prune=true, selfHeal=true           │
  │   - name: anihpj-prod      path: environments/prod/            │
  │     syncPolicy: automated, prune=true  ← Manual sync only      │
  │   - name: anihpj-test      path: environments/test/            │
  │   - name: anihpj-dr        path: environments/dr/              │
  │   - name: anihpj-sandbox   path: environments/sandbox/         │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  ONE sync = ALL 6 environments deployed. Add env = +1 YAML entry.

For anihpj/jobpost: The root App of Apps defines all 6 environments (dev, staging, prod, test, DR, sandbox). Syncing the root app once bootstraps the entire platform. Adding a 7th environment is just one more entry in this YAML file — no manual ArgoCD UI clicks.

GitOps Patterns Q134 of 200
How does the "App of Apps" pattern work for anihpj across environments?
A) The root app (argocd/app-of-apps.yaml) defines 3 child apps: (1) app-anihpj-dev → environments/dev/, (2) app-anihpj-staging → environments/staging/, (3) app-anihpj-prod → environments/prod/. Each child has its own sync policy, RBAC, and health checks. ArgoCD syncs the root app ONCE → all 3 child apps are created → each child syncs its environment. The root app is the "master switch" — adding a DR environment is just one more entry
B) Each environment is a separate ArgoCD installation — App of Apps doesn't apply to anihpj
C) App of Apps only works for a single environment — you need separate clusters per env
D) The root app is defined in anihpj-prod only; dev and staging are manually managed
✅ Correct Answer: A
anihpj App of Apps — The Master Switch
  argocd/app-of-apps.yaml (ROOT — synced ONCE)
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ applications:                                                  │
  │   - name: anihpj-dev       path: environments/dev/             │
  │     syncPolicy: automated, prune=true, selfHeal=true           │
  │     namespace: anihpj-dev                                      │
  │   - name: anihpj-staging   path: environments/staging/         │
  │     syncPolicy: automated, prune=true                          │
  │     namespace: anihpj-staging                                  │
  │   - name: anihpj-prod      path: environments/prod/            │
  │     syncPolicy: automated, prune=true, selfHeal=true           │
  │     namespace: anihpj-prod                                     │
  │     ⚠️ Manual sync only (Continuous Delivery, not Deployment)   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  ONE sync = ALL 3 envs deployed. Add DR env = +1 entry. "Master switch."

The root app is the "master switch." Adding a 4th environment is just adding one more entry to the root app's YAML. No manual ArgoCD CLI or UI steps needed.

🔬 Adding a New Environment — One YAML Entry
Adding anihpj-dr — Just One More Entry
  BEFORE (5 environments):                    AFTER (6 environments):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ applications:              │            │ applications:              │
  │   - name: anihpj-dev       │            │   - name: anihpj-dev       │
  │   - name: anihpj-staging   │            │   - name: anihpj-staging   │
  │   - name: anihpj-prod      │            │   - name: anihpj-prod      │
  │   - name: anihpj-test      │            │   - name: anihpj-test      │
  │   - name: anihpj-sandbox   │            │   - name: anihpj-sandbox   │
  └────────────────────────────┘            │   - name: anihpj-dr  ← NEW │
                                            │     path: environments/dr/ │
  DIFF: +4 lines of YAML.                   │     syncPolicy: automated  │
  RESULT: 6th environment deployed.         └────────────────────────────┘
  No UI clicks. No CLI commands. Just git commit + push.

For anihpj/jobpost: Adding a new DR environment is 4 lines of YAML in the root App of Apps. Git commit + push → ArgoCD syncs → new environment deployed. No manual steps, no CLI commands, no UI clicks. Fully declarative environment provisioning.

GitOps Patterns Q135 of 200
What is the "ApplicationSet" pattern and how does it differ from "App of Apps"?
A) ApplicationSet is an ArgoCD controller that GENERATES Applications from a template + generator. Generators: Git directories, Git files, List (explicit), Cluster (auto-discover), Matrix (combine). Unlike App of Apps (static list), ApplicationSet is DYNAMIC — it automatically creates an Application for each new cluster, branch, or directory. App of Apps = static, explicit. ApplicationSet = template-based, dynamic, auto-scaling
B) ApplicationSet is the same as App of Apps — just a different name in the ArgoCD docs
C) ApplicationSet is a Flux CD feature; App of Apps is an ArgoCD feature
D) ApplicationSet is for small deployments; App of Apps is for large enterprises
✅ Correct Answer: A
App of Apps (Static) vs ApplicationSet (Dynamic)
  APP OF APPS:                              APPLICATIONSET:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Explicit list of 3 apps:   │            │ Template + Generator:       │
  │   - dev                     │            │   generator:                │
  │   - staging                 │            │     git: dirs in envs/*    │
  │   - prod                    │            │   template: app-{{name}}   │
  │                            │            │                            │
  │ Add env = EDIT YAML         │            │ Add env = ADD DIRECTORY    │
  │ Manual update               │            │ Auto-detected!              │
  │ Fixed count                 │            │ Dynamic count               │
  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘
  App of Apps = static declaration. ApplicationSet = template factory.
  Use ApplicationSet for: many clusters, many tenants, many branches.
  Use App of Apps for: small, fixed set of environments (3-5).

ApplicationSet is template-based, dynamic, auto-scaling. App of Apps is static, explicit. Use ApplicationSet for many similar Applications; App of Apps for small fixed sets.

🔬 ApplicationSet Generators — Which to Use When
ApplicationSet Generator Types — Decision Guide
  GENERATOR       │ USE WHEN                          │ EXAMPLE
  ────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────
  List            │ Fixed set of apps                 │ 6 envs (dev/sta/...
                  │                                   │ prod/test/dr/sand)
  Git Directory   │ One app per directory in repo     │ apps/*/kustomize
  Git Files       │ Apps defined in JSON/YAML in repo │ apps.json list
  Cluster         │ Auto-discover registered clusters │ New cluster added
                  │                                   │ → auto-creates app
  Matrix          │ Combine 2+ generators             │ Git Dirs × Clusters
  SCM Provider    │ One app per PR/branch             │ Preview environments
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  For anihpj: List generator (6 fixed envs) or Git Directory (1 per dir).

For anihpj/jobpost: Use List generator for the 6 fixed environments (simple, explicit). Use Git Directory generator if adding per-service directories. Use SCM Provider generator for PR preview environments. ApplicationSet adapts as the platform grows.

GitOps Patterns Q136 of 200
Mono-repo vs Multi-repo — which pattern for GitOps?
A) Mono-repo: ONE Git repo with all environments as subdirectories (environments/dev/, staging/, prod/). Simpler, easier cross-env visibility, atomic cross-env changes. Multi-repo: ONE repo PER environment (dev.anihpj-gitops, prod.anihpj-gitops). Independent access control, independent CI, reduced blast radius if a repo is compromised. Many teams start mono-repo and split to multi-repo as they grow — there's no universal "best"
B) Mono-repo is always better — multi-repo is an anti-pattern in GitOps
C) ArgoCD only supports mono-repo; Flux only supports multi-repo
D) Multi-repo is required for GitOps certification; mono-repo will fail the exam
✅ Correct Answer: A
Mono-repo vs Multi-repo — Decision Framework
  MONO-REPO                                  MULTI-REPO
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
  │ anihpj-gitops/             │            │ dev-repo │ │stag-repo │ │prod-repo │
  │ ├── dev/                   │            │ (dev/)   │ │(staging/)│ │(prod/)   │
  │ ├── staging/               │            └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘
  │ └── prod/                  │
  └────────────────────────────┘            PROS: Independent ACLs, CI, blast radius
  PROS: Simple, atomic, visible             CONS: More repos, harder cross-env view
  CONS: Single ACL, shared CI               START: Mono → GROW: Split to multi
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  For anihpj (small team, 3 envs): Mono-repo. For 50+ envs across 10 teams: Multi-repo.

No universal "best." Mono-repo = simpler, better for small teams. Multi-repo = better isolation, better for large teams. Many teams start mono-repo and split to multi-repo as they scale.

🔬 When to Split — Mono → Multi Repo Triggers
Mono-Repo → Multi-Repo — Migration Triggers
  TRIGGER                          │ ACTION
  ─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────
  Team grows beyond 5 engineers    │ Split prod to own repo (ACL)
  Compliance requires separation   │ Split prod to own repo (audit)
  of duties for production         │
  ─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────
  CI pipeline time > 10 min        │ Split to per-env repos (parallel)
  (scanning all envs on every PR)  │
  ─────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────
  Frequent merge conflicts         │ Split by team/service ownership
  between teams editing same repo  │
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  For anihpj: Currently mono-repo (3 envs, small team). Split when
  the team reaches 5+ engineers or compliance requires prod isolation.

For anihpj/jobpost: Start with mono-repo (simple, 3 environments, small team). Split to multi-repo when the team grows beyond 5 engineers, or when compliance requires strict separation of duties for production access. Don't prematurely optimize — mono-repo works fine at small scale.

GitOps Patterns Q137 of 200
What is the "Environment per Branch" pattern (Preview Environments)?
A) Each Git branch automatically gets its own K8s namespace/environment deployed. A PR to the main branch triggers creation of a "preview environment" (e.g., pr-342.anihpj.dev) with the PR's changes. This allows reviewing changes LIVE before merging. After PR merge, the preview env is cleaned up. Tools: ArgoCD ApplicationSet with SCM Provider generator, Flux's Branch Planner, or GitLab Review Apps
B) Each developer has their own Kubernetes cluster — GitOps manages per-cluster configs
C) Environment per branch means production runs on the main branch; everything else is staging
D) This pattern is unsupported in GitOps — branches should not be used for environments
✅ Correct Answer: A
Preview Environments — Review Changes LIVE Before Merge
  DEV OPENS PR #342:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ GitHub PR: "Fix API pagination bug"                             │
  │ ApplicationSet detects new branch → creates:                    │
  │   Namespace: pr-342-anihpj                                      │
  │   URL: https://pr-342.anihpj.dev                                │
  │   ArgoCD deploys branch version                                 │
  │                                                                 │
  │ Reviewer visits pr-342.anihpj.dev → VERIFIES fix LIVE           │
  │ PR merged → ApplicationSet cleans up namespace, DNS, resources  │
  │ PR closed → preview env destroyed automatically                 │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  "Test in production-like env BEFORE merging." Live, not just code review.

Preview environments allow reviewing changes LIVE before merging. ApplicationSet auto-creates and auto-destroys per-branch environments. This increases confidence in PRs.

🔬 Preview Environments — anihpj PR Review Workflow
PR #342 — Preview Environment Lifecycle
  DEVELOPER OPENS PR #342:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. ApplicationSet detects new branch: feat/new-checkout        │
  │ 2. Creates: namespace pr-342-anihpj                            │
  │ 3. Deploys: Django API + PostgreSQL + Redis (full stack)       │
  │ 4. DNS: pr-342.anihpj.dev → Ingress → preview pods            │
  │                                                                │
  │ REVIEWER visits pr-342.anihpj.dev → tests new feature LIVE     │
  │ → Approves PR → merges to main                                 │
  │                                                                │
  │ 5. PR merged → ApplicationSet:                                 │
  │    • Deletes namespace pr-342-anihpj                           │
  │    • Removes DNS record                                         │
  │    • Cleans up all preview resources                           │
  │                                                                │
  │ COST: ~$0.50/hr while PR is open. Auto-cleanup on merge.      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  For anihpj: Preview envs on kind-cl3. Auto-created. Auto-destroyed.

For anihpj/jobpost: Use ApplicationSet with SCM Provider generator on kind-cl3 for PR preview environments. Each PR gets a full-stack preview (Django + PostgreSQL + Redis) accessible via pr-NNN.anihpj.dev. Reviewers test changes LIVE before approving. Auto-cleanup on merge saves resources.

GitOps Patterns Q138 of 200
How does the Kustomize overlay pattern work for multi-environment GitOps?
A) A BASE directory contains common manifests valid for all environments. Each environment has an OVERLAY directory that patches ONLY the fields that differ (replicas, image tag, LOG_LEVEL, resource limits). Kustomize applies the overlay patches on top of the base, producing the final env-specific YAML. This is DRY — common config is written once, differences are only what varies. ArgoCD/Flux render the overlay at sync time
B) Each environment has completely separate YAML files with full duplication
C) Kustomize doesn't support multi-environment — you need Helm for that
D) The overlay pattern requires a separate Git repo per environment
✅ Correct Answer: A
Kustomize Overlay = Base + Patches = DRY GitOps
  BASE/                       DEV/                        PROD/
  ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────┐
  │ deploy.yaml:      │       │ kustomization.yaml│       │ kustomization.yaml│
  │   replicas: 1     │       │ patches:          │       │ patches:          │
  │   image: api:v1   │       │   replicas: 1 ✅   │       │   replicas: 3     │
  │                    │       │   LOG: DEBUG       │       │   LOG: WARN      │
  │ configmap.yaml:   │       │                    │       │   limits.cpu: 1  │
  │   LOG_LEVEL: INFO │       │                    │       │                    │
  └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘       └──────────────────┘
         ↓                            ↓                            ↓
  COMMON TO ALL              ONLY REPLICAS=1              ONLY replicas=3
                             (no change needed)           LOG_LEVEL=WARN
  Kustomize merges: BASE + overlay = final YAML. Same base, different patches.
  Write once. Override only differences. DRY (Don't Repeat Yourself).

Kustomize overlay = write once, override only where needed. Base has common config. Overlays have only the differences. Both ArgoCD and Flux support Kustomize natively.

🔬 anihpj Kustomize Structure — Complete Directory Layout
anihpj Kustomize — Base + Overlays = Multi-Environment
  environments/
  ├── base/                          ← Common (ALL envs)
  │   ├── kustomization.yaml         ← Lists all resources
  │   ├── deploy.yaml                ← Template: replicas, image, ports
  │   ├── svc.yaml                   ← Service: ClusterIP, port 8000
  │   ├── configmap.yaml             ← LOG_LEVEL=INFO, DB_HOST, etc.
  │   ├── hpa.yaml                   ← minReplicas, maxReplicas, CPU%
  │   └── network-policy.yaml        ← Default-deny + app rules
  ├── dev/                           ← Overlay: DEBUG, 100m CPU
  │   └── kustomization.yaml         ← patches + nameSuffix: -dev
  ├── staging/                       ← Overlay: INFO, 250m CPU, replicas:2
  │   └── kustomization.yaml
  └── prod/                          ← Overlay: WARN, 500m CPU, replicas:3
      └── kustomization.yaml
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Base = ~50 lines. Each overlay = ~8 lines. DRY. Auditable. Versioned.

For anihpj/jobpost: The base directory contains all common Kubernetes resources (~50 lines). Each environment overlay adds only ~8 lines of differences (replicas, CPU, log level). ArgoCD runs `kustomize build environments/prod` at sync time — producing the final, complete Kubernetes YAML.

GitOps Patterns Q139 of 200
What is the Helm Umbrella Chart pattern for GitOps?
A) An Umbrella Chart is a Helm chart that contains MULTIPLE sub-charts as dependencies. For anihpj: umbrella chart "anihpj-stack" depends on: django-api (v1.2.4), postgresql (v14.2.0), redis (v7.0.0), nginx-ingress (v1.8.0). One values.yaml controls all sub-charts. One git commit deploys the ENTIRE stack. This is powerful for deploying multi-service applications as a unit. ArgoCD/Flux render the umbrella chart at sync time
B) Umbrella charts are deprecated — Helm no longer supports them
C) Umbrella charts only work with Flux, not ArgoCD
D) Each sub-chart must be deployed as a separate ArgoCD Application
✅ Correct Answer: A
Helm Umbrella Chart = Entire Stack in One Deploy
  anihpj-stack/ (Umbrella Chart)
  ├── Chart.yaml (dependencies)
  │   ├── django-api    v1.2.4  (custom app)
  │   ├── postgresql    v14.2.0 (Bitnami chart)
  │   ├── redis         v7.0.0  (Bitnami chart)
  │   └── nginx-ingress v1.8.0  (NGINX chart)
  └── values.yaml
      ├── django-api.replicas: 3
      ├── postgresql.storage: 20Gi
      ├── redis.password: secret://...
      └── nginx-ingress.host: jobpost.com

  ONE ArgoCD Application → ONE umbrella chart → ENTIRE stack deployed as a unit.
  Single source of truth for the WHOLE application stack. Atomic deploy/rollback.

Umbrella charts bundle multi-service applications. One values.yaml controls everything. One git commit = entire stack deploy/rollback. Great for microservice architectures.

🔬 anihpj Umbrella Chart — Complete Stack Definition
anihpj-stack Umbrella Chart — All Dependencies
  anihpj-stack/Chart.yaml:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ dependencies:                                                  │
  │   - name: django-api       version: 1.2.4  (custom app)       │
  │   - name: postgresql       version: 14.2.0 (Bitnami)          │
  │   - name: redis            version: 7.0.0  (Bitnami)          │
  │   - name: nginx-ingress    version: 1.8.0  (NGINX)            │
  │   - name: cert-manager     version: 1.12.0 (Jetstack)         │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  ONE helm install = ENTIRE stack. ONE values.yaml = ALL config.
  ONE git commit = atomic deploy/rollback of everything.

For anihpj/jobpost: The umbrella chart bundles all 5 components (API, DB, cache, ingress, certs) into a single deployable unit. One `helm install` deploys the entire stack. One git commit rolls back everything atomically. Perfect for tightly-coupled microservices.

GitOps Patterns Q140 of 200
What is the "Fan-Out" pattern in GitOps?
A) ONE Git repo → MANY clusters. A single Git repository contains manifests that are deployed to multiple Kubernetes clusters. ArgoCD/Flux on each cluster watches the SAME repo (possibly different paths/branches). Example: one anihpj-gitops repo → deployed to dev cluster (kind), staging cluster (kind), prod cluster (kubeadm), DR cluster (EKS). Consistency: all clusters read from the same source of truth
B) Many Git repos → one cluster (the opposite of fan-out)
C) Fan-out requires separate reconciler instances per Git repo
D) Fan-out is only for CI pipelines, not GitOps
✅ Correct Answer: A
Fan-Out: One Git → Many Clusters
                        ┌────────────────────┐
                        │ anihpj-gitops repo │
                        │ (Single source)    │
                        └──────┬─────────────┘
                               │
               ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐
               ▼               ▼               ▼
        ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐
        │ Dev      │    │ Staging  │    │ Prod     │
        │ (kind)   │    │ (kind)   │    │ (kubeadm)│
        │ path:dev/│    │path:stag/│    │path:prod/│
        └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘

  ONE repo. MANY clusters. Each reads different path. Same source of truth.
  Consistency: what's deployed on prod = what's in Git (verifiable).

Fan-out: one Git repo, many clusters. Each cluster's reconciler watches the same repo. Benefits: consistency, simplicity, auditability.

🔬 Fan-Out — anihpj 6-Cluster Implementation
anihpj Fan-Out — One Repo → Six Clusters
                          anihpj-gitops (ONE repo)
                                  │
          ┌───────────────────────┼───────────────────────┐
          │           │           │           │           │
          ▼           ▼           ▼           ▼           ▼
    ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐
    │kubeadm  │ │kind-cl1 │ │kind-cl2 │ │kind-cl3 │ │minikube │
    │(prod)   │ │(dev)    │ │(staging)│ │(test)   │ │(sandbox)│
    │path:prod│ │path:dev │ │path:stag│ │path:test│ │path:sand│
    └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘
  ONE repo. SIX clusters. Each reads its own path. Independent sync.
  All clusters share the same source of truth = consistency guaranteed.

For anihpj/jobpost: All 6 clusters pull from the same anihpj-gitops repo. Each reads a different path (prod/, dev/, staging/, etc.). Changing dev/ only affects kind-cl1 — no cross-contamination. One source of truth, consistent across all environments.

GitOps Patterns Q141 of 200
What is the "Fan-In" pattern in GitOps?
A) MANY Git repos → ONE cluster (via App of Apps). A single cluster runs an ArgoCD instance that watches MULTIPLE Git repos. Each repo contains manifests for a different service/team. The root App of Apps points to these repos. Benefit: each team owns their own deployment repo; the cluster operator provides the platform (ArgoCD). Federation: each team controls their own deployment cadence within shared infrastructure
B) One Git repo → many clusters (the opposite of fan-in)
C) Fan-in requires separate clusters per Git repo
D) Fan-in is an anti-pattern — it creates deployment conflicts
✅ Correct Answer: A
Fan-In: Many Git Repos → One Cluster
  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
  │ Team A repo  │  │ Team B repo  │  │ Team C repo  │
  │ (django-api) │  │ (web-front)  │  │ (analytics)  │
  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘
         │                 │                 │
         └─────────────────┼─────────────────┘
                           ▼
                  ┌─────────────────┐
                  │ ArgoCD (cluster)│ ← Platform team provides this
                  │ App of Apps     │
                  │ watches all     │
                  └─────────────────┘
  Each team OWNS their repo. Platform team PROVIDES ArgoCD + cluster.
  Independent deploy cadence. Shared infrastructure. Federation pattern.

Fan-in: many repos, one cluster. Each team owns their deployment repo. Platform team provides ArgoCD and infrastructure. Independent deploy cadences within shared infrastructure.

🔬 Federation Pattern — Multi-Team anihpj Platform
Fan-In Federation — Teams Own Repos, Platform Owns ArgoCD
  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐
  │ Team A repo  │  │ Team B repo  │  │ Team C repo  │
  │ (django-api) │  │ (web-front)  │  │ (analytics)  │
  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘
         └─────────────────┼─────────────────┘
                           ▼
                  ┌─────────────────┐
                  │ ArgoCD (kubeadm)│ ← Platform team OWNs
                  │ App of Apps     │
                  │ watches ALL     │
                  └─────────────────┘
  Team A deploys independently. Team B deploys independently.
  Platform team provides ArgoCD + cluster. Federation pattern.

For anihpj/jobpost: As the platform grows, different teams (API team, frontend team, analytics team) own their own Git repos. The platform team provides ArgoCD as a shared service. Each team deploys independently within their namespace — federation without chaos.

GitOps Patterns Q142 of 200
How do you bootstrap GitOps in a new cluster?
A) Bootstrap = the INITIAL installation that sets up GitOps on an empty cluster. Steps: (1) Install ArgoCD/Flux via CLI or Helm, (2) Point it at your Git repo (App of Apps or Flux bootstrap), (3) The bootstrap Application creates all other Applications. After bootstrap, the GitOps tool manages ITSELF through Git — the bootstrap Application is itself tracked in Git. Result: cluster bootstrapped from zero; all config in Git; self-managing
B) Bootstrap means manually applying all YAML files with kubectl — GitOps starts after
C) Bootstrap is only needed for production clusters — dev clusters don't use GitOps
D) ArgoCD doesn't support bootstrapping — you must manually create each Application
✅ Correct Answer: A
GitOps Bootstrap — From Empty Cluster to Self-Managing
  EMPTY CLUSTER:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ STEP 1: argocd app create bootstrap \                          │
  │           --repo anihpj-gitops --path argocd/ \                │
  │           --dest-server https://kubernetes.default.svc         │
  │                                                                │
  │ STEP 2: ArgoCD creates:                                        │
  │   - app-of-apps (root)                                         │
  │   - anihpj-dev, anihpj-staging, anihpj-prod (children)         │
  │   - All namespace, RBAC, ConfigMap, Secret, Deploy resources   │
  │                                                                │
  │ STEP 3: ArgoCD now manages ITSELF.                              │
  │   The bootstrap Application is itself in Git.                  │
  │   Changes to ArgoCD config → commit to Git → auto-syncs.       │
  │                                                                │
  │ RESULT: Cluster bootstrapped from scratch. All in Git.          │
  │         Reproducible. Self-managing. Disaster-proof.            │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Bootstrap = initial setup from zero. After bootstrap, GitOps manages itself. The bootstrap Application is tracked in Git — changes to ArgoCD config are committed to Git and auto-synced.

🔬 anihpj Bootstrap — From Empty Cluster to Production
Bootstrap Timeline — Zero to Production in 10 Minutes
  T+0:00  kubeadm init (empty cluster, no workloads)
  T+1:00  kubectl apply -f https://.../argocd-install.yaml
  T+2:00  ArgoCD pods running (argocd namespace)
  T+3:00  kubectl apply -f argocd/app-of-apps.yaml
  T+4:00  Root app: OutOfSync → Syncing → Progressing
  T+5:00  Child apps created: anihpj-dev, staging, prod, test, DR, sand
  T+7:00  All child apps syncing manifests from Git
  T+9:00  Health checks passing (pods Ready, probes OK)
  T+10:00 ALL apps: Synced + Healthy ✅
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  RESULT: Fully deployed anihpj/jobpost platform from Git alone.
  Reproducible. Self-documenting. Disaster-proof.

For anihpj/jobpost: An empty cluster becomes a fully deployed production platform in ~10 minutes. The bootstrap App of Apps in Git IS the rebuild plan. If the cluster burns down, bootstrap again from Git. Reproducible infrastructure as code.

4.2 Progressive Delivery PatternsQ143–Q152

GitOps Patterns Q143 of 200
What is "Progressive Delivery" and how does GitOps enable it?
A) Progressive Delivery = gradually rolling out changes to a subset of users, monitoring health, and automatically promoting or rolling back based on metrics. GitOps enables this via: Argo Rollouts (canary, blue-green), Flagger (progressive delivery operator). The Git state defines the rollout STAGES; the progressive delivery controller orchestrates the progression. Example: "deploy to 5% → check Prometheus → if healthy, go to 25% → check → 100%. If metrics degrade, auto-rollback" — all defined declaratively in Git
B) Progressive Delivery is the same as Continuous Delivery — no difference
C) Progressive Delivery requires manual approval at every stage — it cannot be automated
D) GitOps doesn't support Progressive Delivery — you need a separate CI/CD tool
✅ Correct Answer: A
Progressive Delivery = Gradual + Metric-Driven
  TRADITIONAL DEPLOY:                        PROGRESSIVE DELIVERY:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 100% traffic switch        │            │ 5% → check metrics ✅       │
  │ "All or nothing"           │            │ 25% → check metrics ✅      │
  │ If broken → 100% affected  │            │ 100% → check metrics ✅     │
  │                            │            │                            │
  │ Rollback: manual           │            │ If ANY step fails →         │
  │                            │            │ AUTO-ROLLBACK to previous   │
  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘
  Git declares: "canary with these steps + these metrics."
  Argo Rollouts/Flagger executes + reports status back to Git via reconciler.

Progressive delivery = "deploy to 5% → check metrics → if healthy, go to 25% → check → 100%." If metrics degrade → auto-rollback. Fully declarative, fully automated.

🔬 Progressive Delivery Pipeline — Canary Step by Step
Canary Deployment — 5% → 25% → 100% with Metric Gates
  STEP │ TRAFFIC │ DURATION │ METRIC CHECK           │ ACTION
  ─────┼─────────┼──────────┼────────────────────────┼──────────
  1    │ 5%      │ 5 min    │ errorRate < 1%         │ PASS → 2
      │         │          │ p99 < 500ms            │ FAIL → ROLLBACK
  2    │ 25%     │ 5 min    │ errorRate < 1%         │ PASS → 3
      │         │          │ p99 < 500ms            │ FAIL → ROLLBACK
  3    │ 100%    │ —        │ errorRate < 1%         │ PROMOTED ✅
      │         │          │ p99 < 500ms            │
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  IF ANY STEP FAILS → AUTO-ROLLBACK to previous stable version.
  The canary process IS CODE (YAML in Git). Not a manual procedure.

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Argo Rollouts for the Django API canary deployments. 5% traffic for 5 minutes → check Prometheus metrics → 25% for 5 minutes → check → 100%. If error rate exceeds 1% or p99 latency exceeds 500ms at any step, auto-rollback to the previous version.

GitOps Patterns Q144 of 200
How does Argo Rollouts implement a Canary deployment for anihpj-api?
A) Instead of a standard Deployment, anihpj-api uses an Argo Rollout resource. The Rollout defines: (1) Canary steps: setWeight: 10% → pause 5 min → setWeight: 50% → pause 5 min → setWeight: 100%. (2) Analysis template: query Prometheus for error rate < 1% AND p99 latency < 500ms. (3) If analysis passes → promote to next step. If analysis fails → auto-rollback. All defined declaratively in Git; all reconciled by ArgoCD + Argo Rollouts controller
B) Argo Rollouts is a separate tool that replaces ArgoCD for canary deployments
C) Canary deployments require manual HTTP header routing — Argo Rollouts can't automate this
D) Argo Rollouts only supports blue-green, not canary deployments
✅ Correct Answer: A
Argo Rollouts Canary — Declarative Progressive Delivery
  anihpj-api-rollout.yaml (in Git, tracked by ArgoCD):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ kind: Rollout                                                  │
  │ spec:                                                          │
  │   strategy:                                                    │
  │     canary:                                                    │
  │       steps:                                                   │
  │         - setWeight: 10   → 10% traffic to new version         │
  │         - pause: {duration: 5m}  → Wait, watch metrics         │
  │         - analysis: { errorRate<1%, p99<500ms } → CHECK         │
  │         - setWeight: 50   → 50% traffic                        │
  │         - pause: {duration: 5m}                                 │
  │         - analysis: { errorRate<1%, p99<500ms } → CHECK         │
  │         - setWeight: 100  → 100% traffic (full promotion)      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  IF ANY ANALYSIS FAILS → AUTO-ROLLBACK to previous version.
  The canary process IS CODE (a YAML file in Git), not a manual procedure.

Git defines EVERYTHING: canary steps, pause durations, metric checks. No manual process. No "let me check Grafana and decide." The Rollout YAML IS the canary procedure.

🔬 anihpj Rollout YAML — Production Canary Configuration
anihpj-api Rollout — Declarative Progressive Delivery
  apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
  kind: Rollout
  spec:
    strategy:
      canary:
        steps:
          - setWeight: 10
          - pause: {duration: 5m}
          - analysis:
              templates:
                - templateName: error-rate-check
          - setWeight: 50
          - pause: {duration: 5m}
          - analysis:
              templates:
                - templateName: latency-check
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  ANALYSIS TEMPLATE (error-rate-check):
    metrics:
      - name: error-rate
        successCondition: result < 0.01
        provider: prometheus
        query: sum(rate(errors[5m]))/sum(rate(requests[5m]))
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Git defines EVERYTHING: canary steps, pause durations, metric checks.
  No manual process. No "let me check Grafana and decide."

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django API Rollout YAML in Git defines the complete canary process — traffic percentages, pause durations, and Prometheus metric queries. Argo Rollouts executes it automatically. The canary process is auditable, versionable, and reproducible — because it's code.

GitOps Patterns Q145 of 200
What is the difference between Blue-Green and Canary deployments in GitOps?
A) Blue-Green: Two complete environments (Blue = current, Green = new). Traffic switches ALL at once from Blue → Green. Instant rollback (switch back). Requires 2x resources during transition. Canary: Gradually shifts a PERCENTAGE of traffic from old to new in steps (5% → 25% → 100%). Slower but catches problems with minimal blast radius. No extra environment needed (just extra pods). Both defined declaratively in Argo Rollouts YAML in Git
B) Blue-Green is for Kubernetes; Canary is for serverless only
C) Canary deployments have instant rollback; Blue-Green requires manual intervention
D) GitOps only supports Blue-Green — Canary requires a separate service mesh
✅ Correct Answer: A
Blue-Green vs Canary — Both Declarative in Git
  BLUE-GREEN:                                CANARY:
  ┌────────────────────┐                    ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ BLUE (v1) GREEN(v2)│                    │ v1 (90%) ──▶ v2 (10%)     │
  │         ↓          │                    │ v1 (50%) ──▶ v2 (50%)     │
  │ BLUE      GREEN(v2)│                    │ v1 (0%)  ──▶ v2 (100%)    │
  │  100% traffic      │                    │                            │
  └────────────────────┘                    └────────────────────────────┘
  PROS: Instant rollback                    PROS: Gradual validation
  CONS: 2x resources during switch          CONS: Slower rollout
  BEST FOR: Critical infra, DB migrations   BEST FOR: User-facing services
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Both = Argo Rollout YAML in Git. Choose based on risk tolerance & resources.

Blue-Green: instant rollback, 2x resources. Best for critical systems and DB migrations. Canary: gradual validation, minimal blast radius. Best for user-facing services. Both are defined declaratively in the Rollout YAML in Git. Choose based on risk tolerance and resource constraints.

🔬 Choosing Blue-Green vs Canary — Decision Matrix for anihpj
Blue-Green vs Canary — When to Use Which
  CRITERIA                │ BLUE-GREEN          │ CANARY
  ────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────────
  Rollback speed          │ Instant (< 1 sec)   │ Gradual (minutes)
  Resource cost           │ 2x during switch    │ ~1.1x (extra pods)
  Blast radius            │ 100% users at once  │ 5% → 25% → 100%
  Validation time         │ ~1-2 min (health)   │ ~10-15 min (steps)
  Best for                │ DB migrations       │ User-facing APIs
  │ Infra changes        │ Web applications    │
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  For anihpj: DB migrations → Blue-Green (instant rollback if
  migration fails). Django API → Canary (gradual user validation).
  Both defined in same Rollout YAML. Both triggered by Git commit.

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Blue-Green for DB migrations (instant rollback if migration corrupts data). Use Canary for the Django API (gradual validation with real users, minimal blast radius). Both strategies live in the same Argo Rollouts YAML in Git. Pick the right tool for each workload.

GitOps Patterns Q146 of 200
How does Flagger integrate with ArgoCD/Flux for automated canary?
A) Flagger is a Progressive Delivery Operator that works ALONGSIDE ArgoCD/Flux: (1) ArgoCD/Flux manages the Rollout/Canary CRD from Git. (2) Flagger WATCHES these CRDs and executes the progressive delivery: shifts traffic, runs metric analysis, promotes or rolls back. (3) Flagger reports status back to the CRD → ArgoCD/Flux sees the status. ArgoCD/Flux = manages desired state. Flagger = executes the progressive delivery STEPS
B) Flagger replaces ArgoCD — you use one or the other
C) Flagger only works with Flux; ArgoCD uses Argo Rollouts instead
D) Flagger is a CI tool, not a CD tool — it handles builds, not deployments
✅ Correct Answer: A
ArgoCD/Flux + Flagger = Declarative + Progressive
  GIT (Rollout/Canary CRD)
       │
       ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ ArgoCD/Flux                │ ← Manages desired state (from Git)
  │ Applies Rollout CRD         │
  └──────────┬─────────────────┘
             │
             ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Flagger                     │ ← Executes progressive delivery
  │ 1. Reads Canary CRD         │
  │ 2. Shifts traffic (5%→50%) │
  │ 3. Runs metric analysis     │   Prometheus ← Queries metrics
  │ 4. Promotes OR Rollback     │
  │ 5. Reports status to CRD    │
  └────────────────────────────┘
  ArgoCD/Flux = "WHAT to deploy." Flagger = "HOW to deploy it safely."

ArgoCD/Flux manages the desired state (declarative). Flagger executes the progressive delivery (HOW to transition safely). They work together: Git defines the canary CRD → ArgoCD applies it → Flagger executes the progression → reports status back.

🔬 Flagger + ArgoCD — anihpj Integration Architecture
Flagger Canary Lifecycle — 6 Stages, All Automated
  STAGE │ FLAGGER ACTION                 │ TRAFFIC │ METRICS
  ──────┼────────────────────────────────┼─────────┼──────────
  1     │ Detect new image tag change    │ v1:100% │ Baseline
  2     │ Scale up canary pods (v2)      │ v1:100% │ Warming up
  3     │ Shift 5% → v2                  │ v1:95%  │ Collecting
  4     │ Run metric analysis (5 min)    │ v1:95%  │ errorRate
  5     │ Shift 50% → v2 (if passed)     │ v1:50%  │ Collecting
  6     │ Run metric analysis (5 min)    │ v1:50%  │ p99 check
  ──▶   │ PROMOTE: 100% → v2             │ v2:100% │ ✅
  OR    │ ROLLBACK: 100% → v1            │ v1:100% │ ❌
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  For anihpj: ArgoCD watches Git repo. Flagger watches ArgoCD's
  Rollout CRDs. Together = declarative source + progressive execution.

For anihpj/jobpost: Install Flagger alongside ArgoCD on kubeadm cluster. ArgoCD syncs the Rollout YAML from Git. Flagger detects the new image tag and automatically executes the 6-stage canary lifecycle. ArgoCD = desired state. Flagger = progressive execution engine.

GitOps Patterns Q147 of 200
How does A/B testing with GitOps work (header-based routing vs percentage)?
A) A/B testing routes users to different versions based on HEADERS (e.g., X-Experiment-Group: A) or cookies — NOT percentage. Different from canary: canary = random % gets new version; A/B = specific users get specific versions. GitOps pattern: Argo Rollouts + Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd) defines header-based routing rules in Git. The Rollout references a VirtualService that routes "group=A" → v1, "group=B" → v2
B) A/B testing and canary deployments are the same thing
C) A/B testing requires a separate feature flag service — GitOps cannot manage it
D) A/B testing in GitOps requires manual header configuration on the load balancer
✅ Correct Answer: A
A/B Testing vs Canary — Different Routing Logic
  CANARY (Percentage-based):                A/B TESTING (Header-based):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 10% random → v2            │            │ X-Group: A → v1 (old)      │
  │ 90% random → v1            │            │ X-Group: B → v2 (new)      │
  │ Users get either randomly  │            │ Users CONSISTENTLY get one │
  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘
  Git defines VirtualService (Istio):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ - match: [headers: {X-Experiment-Group: "B"}]                  │
  │   route: [destination: {host: api, subset: v2}]                │
  │ - route: [destination: {host: api, subset: v1}]  (default)     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  A/B = stickiness (same user → same version). Canary = randomness.

A/B testing routes based on headers/cookies (sticky). Canary routes based on percentage (random). Both are defined declaratively in Git via Argo Rollouts + Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd). The routing rules ARE code in Git.

🔬 A/B Testing — anihpj Experiment with Istio VirtualService
A/B Testing anihpj — Header-Based User Segmentation
  REQUEST FLOW:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ User Alice: X-Experiment-Group: "A" → Istio → anihpj-api v1   │
  │ User Bob:   X-Experiment-Group: "B" → Istio → anihpj-api v2   │
  │ User Carol: (no header)           → Istio → anihpj-api v1 (dflt)│
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  VirtualService (in Git):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ http:                                                          │
  │   - match:                                                     │
  │       - headers:                                               │
  │           X-Experiment-Group:                                  │
  │             exact: "B"                                         │
  │     route:                                                     │
  │       - destination:                                           │
  │           host: anihpj-api                                     │
  │           subset: v2        ← New checkout experience          │
  │   - route:                                                     │
  │       - destination:                                           │
  │           host: anihpj-api                                     │
  │           subset: v1        ← Default (control group)          │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  For anihpj: Test new checkout UI (v2) with 10% of users (Group B).
  Compare conversion rates: v1 vs v2. Promote winner via Git commit.

For anihpj/jobpost: Use Istio VirtualService for A/B testing the new checkout UI. Group B users get v2 (new experience). Group A users get v1 (control). Compare conversion rates between groups. The winner is promoted by a Git commit updating the VirtualService — fully auditable experiment management.

GitOps Patterns Q148 of 200
How to use Prometheus metrics as canary health criteria in GitOps?
A) Argo Rollouts AnalysisTemplate defines Prometheus queries as success/failure conditions. Example: (1) errorRate = sum(rate(http_errors[1m])) / sum(rate(http_requests[1m])), condition: < 1%. (2) p99Latency = histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_req_ms[1m])), condition: < 500ms. (3) successRate > 99%. If ALL conditions pass → promote to next canary step. If ANY fail → auto-rollback. The AnalysisTemplate is a YAML file in Git — the canary health criteria ARE code
B) Prometheus metrics are monitored separately — GitOps doesn't integrate with observability
C) Canary health is determined by CPU/memory metrics only — not application-level metrics
D) Argo Rollouts uses its own metrics system — Prometheus integration is not supported
✅ Correct Answer: A
Prometheus Metrics = Canary Health Check (as Code)
  AnalysisTemplate (in Git):                PROMETHEUS QUERIES:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ metrics:                                                        │
  │   - name: error-rate                                           │
  │     provider: prometheus                                       │
  │     query: |                                                    │
  │       sum(rate(http_errors{app="anihpj-api"}[1m])) /           │
  │       sum(rate(http_requests{app="anihpj-api"}[1m]))           │
  │     successCondition: result < 0.01   ← < 1% error rate        │
  │                                                                │
  │   - name: p99-latency                                          │
  │     provider: prometheus                                       │
  │     query: |                                                    │
  │       histogram_quantile(0.99,                                 │
  │         rate(http_req_ms{app="anihpj-api"}[1m]))               │
  │     successCondition: result < 500    ← < 500ms                │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  PROMOTION: ALL conditions pass → next step. ROLLBACK: ANY fails → previous.
  Canary health criteria ARE CODE. In Git. Reviewable. Versionable.

Canary health criteria ARE Prometheus queries in Git. Error rate < 1%, p99 latency < 500ms, success rate > 99%. If all pass → promote. If any fail → auto-rollback. The criteria are code — reviewable, versionable, auditable.

🔬 anihpj AnalysisTemplate — Complete Prometheus Canary Gate
anihpj Django API — Canary Health Gate (Prometheus)
  METRIC            │ PROMETHEUS QUERY                    │ CONDITION
  ──────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┼──────────
  Error Rate        │ sum(rate(errors[1m])) /             │ < 1%
                    │ sum(rate(requests[1m]))             │
  P99 Latency       │ histogram_quantile(0.99,            │ < 500ms
                    │   rate(req_ms[1m]))                 │
  Success Rate      │ sum(rate(success[1m])) /            │ > 99%
                    │ sum(rate(requests[1m]))             │
  DB Query Latency  │ rate(pg_query_ms[1m])               │ < 200ms
  Memory Usage      │ container_memory_usage_bytes        │ < 512Mi
  ──────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┴──────────
  ALL must pass for promotion. ANY failure = auto-rollback.
  The AnalysisTemplate YAML IS the canary gate. In Git. Auditable.
  For anihpj: Custom metrics include DB query latency (Django ORM →
  Prometheus exporter) and checkout success rate (business metric).

For anihpj/jobpost: The AnalysisTemplate in Git defines 5 Prometheus-based health gates. Beyond standard metrics (error rate, latency), include business metrics like checkout success rate and DB query latency. Custom Django Prometheus exporters feed app-level metrics. Canary health = fully automated, fully auditable.

GitOps Patterns Q149 of 200
What are automatic rollback triggers and how do they interact with Git state?
A) Auto-rollback triggers: (1) Metric failure — Prometheus analysis fails (error rate > threshold), (2) Timeout — canary step exceeds max duration without passing, (3) Webhook — external system signals failure (e.g., Datadog monitor alert). When triggered: Argo Rollouts/Flagger automatically routes 100% traffic BACK to the stable version. IMPORTANT: Git still shows the new (failed) version. The reconciliation is in the cluster; Git is updated with the promotion/rollback RESULT by the controller
B) Rollbacks must be triggered manually by reverting the Git commit
C) Automatic rollbacks update Git first, then the cluster — Git is always the trigger
D) Argo Rollouts doesn't support automatic rollbacks — only manual ones
✅ Correct Answer: A
Auto-Rollback Triggers — Cluster Reacts, Git Records
  TRIGGER SOURCES:                           ROLLBACK ACTION:
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. Metric Failure          │───TRIGGER──▶│ Argo Rollouts/Flagger:     │
  │    errorRate > 1%          │            │ Routes 100% → stable v1    │
  │ 2. Analysis Timeout        │            │ Scaledown canary v2 pods   │
  │    Canary step > 5 min     │            │                            │
  │ 3. External Webhook        │            │ STATUS REPORTED:           │
  │    (Datadog/PagerDuty)     │            │ Rollout.status: Degraded   │
  │                            │            │ Rollout.message: "Rollback │
  └────────────────────────────┘            │   due to errorRate > 1%"  │
                                            └────────────────────────────┘
  CLUSTER reacts immediately (within seconds). Git is updated with RESULT.
  Rollback = automatic. Git record = automatic (via controller status update).

Auto-rollback triggers: metric failure, timeout, external webhook. The cluster reacts immediately (within seconds). Git is updated with the RESULT by the controller. Rollback is automatic AND recorded. No human intervention needed in the critical path.

🔬 Rollback Triggers — Full Matrix with anihpj Thresholds
Auto-Rollback Trigger Matrix — anihpj Thresholds
  TRIGGER TYPE   │ SOURCE            │ THRESHOLD          │ RESPONSE
  ───────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────
  Metric Failure │ Prometheus        │ errorRate > 1%     │ Immediate
  │ Prometheus        │ p99 > 500ms        │ Immediate
  │ Prometheus        │ successRate < 99%  │ Immediate
  ───────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────
  Timeout        │ Rollout controller│ Step > 10 min      │ Immediate
  │                   │ without passing     │
  ───────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────
  Webhook        │ Datadog monitor   │ alert fired         │ < 30 sec
  │ PagerDuty         │ incident created    │ < 30 sec
  │ Custom validator  │ integration test    │ < 30 sec
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Git state AFTER rollback:
  • Cluster: 100% traffic → v1 (stable). Immediate (< 1 sec).
  • Rollout.status in cluster: Degraded + reason = "errorRate > 1%"
  • Git: Controller writes rollback event to Rollout CRD status.
  • Next Git commit: Human investigates, fixes, re-deploys.
  For anihpj: Rollback in seconds. Root cause investigation in minutes.

For anihpj/jobpost: Auto-rollback protects production 24/7 without human wake-up calls. If error rate spikes at 3 AM, Argo Rollouts auto-rolls-back within seconds. The on-call engineer investigates the Git status record in the morning. The cluster heals itself; humans investigate later.

GitOps Patterns Q150 of 200
How to handle Progressive Delivery for non-HTTP workloads (gRPC, TCP)?
A) For non-HTTP: (1) Blue-Green works for ANY protocol (traffic switch at Service/endpoint level, not HTTP-level). (2) Canary requires L7 routing → needs protocol-specific support: gRPC can use header-based routing via Envoy/Istio; TCP can use connection-level canary with weighted Services. (3) Argo Rollouts supports gRPC analysis via Prometheus metrics. (4) For simple TCP services, Blue-Green is preferred. The Rollout YAML is protocol-aware via the service mesh configuration
B) Progressive delivery only works with HTTP workloads — gRPC and TCP are not supported
C) Non-HTTP workloads must use manual deployments — they can't participate in GitOps
D) You need a separate deployment tool for gRPC — Argo Rollouts is HTTP-only
✅ Correct Answer: A
Progressive Delivery — Protocol Support Matrix
  PROTOCOL      CANARY?         BLUE-GREEN?     HOW?
  ────────────  ───────────     ───────────     ─────────────────────────
  HTTP/HTTPS    ✅ Yes           ✅ Yes           Istio/Linkerd VirtualService
  gRPC          ✅ Yes           ✅ Yes           Envoy header routing
  TCP           ⚠️ Limited       ✅ BEST          Weighted Service + endpoints
  UDP           ❌ No            ✅ Yes           Blue-Green via Service switch
  WebSocket     ✅ Yes           ✅ Yes           Istio header routing
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Blue-Green = protocol-agnostic (switch at Service level). Always works.
  Canary = needs L7 routing (protocol-aware). Use service mesh for gRPC/WebSocket.
  For TCP/UDP: Blue-Green is the safe choice.

Blue-Green works for ANY protocol (Service-level switch). Canary needs L7 routing — gRPC uses Envoy header routing; TCP uses weighted Services. Argo Rollouts YAML is protocol-aware via service mesh config. For simple TCP: Blue-Green is preferred.

🔬 Non-HTTP Progressive Delivery — anihpj gRPC + TCP Use Cases
Non-HTTP Progressive Delivery — Protocol Strategy Guide
  PROTOCOL  │ STRATEGY    │ HOW                              │ anihpj USE
  ──────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────
  gRPC      │ Canary ✅   │ Envoy header routing             │ Internal
            │             │ (grpc-status, grpc-message)      │ microservice
            │             │ + Prometheus gRPC metrics        │ comms
  ──────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────
  TCP       │ Blue-Green  │ Service selector swap:           │ PostgreSQL
  (Postgres)│ ✅          │ v1 selector → v2 selector        │ proxy
            │             │ (connection drain first)          │
  ──────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────
  Redis     │ Blue-Green  │ Sentinel-controlled failover     │ Session
            │ ✅          │ + Service endpoint swap          │ store
  ──────────┼─────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┼────────────
  WebSocket │ Canary ✅   │ Istio header + connection drain  │ Real-time
            │             │ (sticky sessions required)       │ notifications
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  For anihpj: gRPC between Django and analytics = canary via Envoy.
  PostgreSQL/Redis = Blue-Green (Service selector swap + drain).
  Rollout YAML IS protocol-aware. Git defines the strategy per workload.

For anihpj/jobpost: Internal gRPC services use canary with Envoy header routing + Prometheus gRPC metrics. PostgreSQL and Redis use Blue-Green with connection drain. WebSocket notifications use sticky-session canary. Each workload gets the right progressive delivery strategy — all defined in Git.

GitOps Patterns Q151 of 200
How to handle multi-service progressive delivery (API + DB migration together)?
A) Use Argo Rollouts with sync waves and hooks: (1) Pre-sync hook runs DB migration (Job) BEFORE the canary starts, (2) Canary deploys new API version to 10% traffic, (3) Analysis step verifies: API error rate < 1%, DB query latency < threshold, (4) If healthy, promote to 100%. The Rollout references a PreSync hook for the migration. Git defines BOTH the migration Job AND the canary steps — atomic multi-service deployment
B) Run DB migration manually first, then deploy API separately — progressive delivery doesn't apply to databases
C) Deploy both API and DB simultaneously at 100% — canary doesn't work with multiple services
D) Use separate ArgoCD applications for API and DB — they must be independently versioned
✅ Correct Answer: A

Sync waves + hooks orchestrate multi-service deployments. PreSync runs DB migration first. Canary verifies API + DB together. PostSync can revert migration on rollback. Git commit is atomic — both services deploy as a unit.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Multi-Service Atomic Deployment
Multi-Service Progressive Delivery — Atomic API + DB Migration
  GIT COMMIT (Atomic):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-gitops/                                                 │
  │ ├── db-migration-job.yaml      ← PreSync hook (runs first)     │
  │ ├── django-api-rollout.yaml    ← Canary: 10%→50%→100%          │
  │ └── analysis-template.yaml     ← Checks: API errors + DB perf  │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  EXECUTION ORDER:
  Step 0: PreSync → Job runs DB migration (ALTER TABLE, etc.)
  Step 1: Canary 10% → New API + migrated DB → check metrics ✅
  Step 2: Canary 50% → check metrics ✅
  Step 3: Canary 100% → FULL promotion
  ROLLBACK: If migration breaks, rollback API + revert DB migration (PostSync hook).
  Git commit = atomic: migration + canary deploy. All or nothing.
🔬 ArgoCD Sync Waves & Hooks — The Orchestration Mechanism
Sync Waves Control Multi-Resource Deployment Order
  SYNC WAVE 0 (PreSync):                    SYNC WAVE 1 (Main Sync):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. DB Migration Job        │            │ 4. ConfigMap/Secret         │
  │    (CREATE TABLE, ALTER)   │───────────▶│ 5. PVC/PV (if needed)       │
  │ 2. Schema validation       │            │ 6. Deployment (canary 10%)  │
  │ 3. Data backfill (if any)  │            │ 7. Service + Ingress        │
  └────────────────────────────┘            │ 8. HPA scaler               │
  SYNC WAVE 2 (PostSync):                   │ 9. Canary 50%→100%          │
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            └────────────────────────────┘
  │ 10. Smoke tests            │
  │ 11. Cleanup old resources  │
  │ 12. Notify Slack #deploy   │
  └────────────────────────────┘
  ARGOCD ANNOTATION: argocd.argoproj.io/sync-wave: "0"
  Resources with wave 0 sync first, then wave 1, then wave 2.
  For anihpj: DB migration = wave 0, Django API = wave 1, notification = wave 2.

For anihpj/jobpost: When deploying a schema change (new column in jobpost table), the sync wave ensures the migration Job COMPLETES before the new Django API pods start. Without sync waves, new pods might try to query a column that doesn't exist yet → CrashLoopBackOff. Sync waves prevent this race condition. Always order: data layer first, then application layer.

GitOps Patterns Q152 of 200
Argo Rollouts vs Flagger vs Istio — which progressive delivery tool when?
A) Argo Rollouts: Native to ArgoCD ecosystem, CRD-based (Rollout resource), supports canary + blue-green, AnalysisTemplate for metrics. Flagger: Service-mesh-aware progressive delivery operator, integrates with Istio/Linkerd/NGINX, supports canary + A/B + blue-green, metric-driven promotion. Istio: Service mesh (not a CD tool) — provides the TRAFFIC ROUTING layer that Rollouts/Flagger use. Choose: Rollouts for ArgoCD-native, Flagger for mesh-heavy environments, always use a mesh WITH Rollouts/Flagger for traffic splitting
B) You only need Istio — it handles progressive delivery natively without Argo/Flagger
C) Argo Rollouts and Flagger are the same tool — just different names for different vendors
D) Flagger requires Argo Rollouts — it's an add-on, not a standalone tool
✅ Correct Answer: A

Each tool has a distinct role. Argo Rollouts/Flagger manage the progressive delivery STATE and STEPS. Istio/Linkerd provide the traffic routing CAPABILITY. They work TOGETHER — the Rollout CRD tells the mesh how to split traffic, and the mesh executes the split.

📝 Detailed Explanation — The Progressive Delivery Tool Stack
Progressive Delivery Tool Stack — Who Does What
  LAYER                    TOOL              ROLE
  ───────────────────────  ────────────────  ─────────────────────────────
  Desired State (Git)      ArgoCD / Flux     Declares Rollout CRD from Git
  Progressive Delivery     Argo Rollouts     Executes canary/blue-green steps
                           Flagger           Service-mesh-aware canary/A/B
  Traffic Routing          Istio / Linkerd    Splits % traffic (5%→50%→100%)
                           NGINX Ingress     Header-based routing for A/B
  Metrics / Analysis       Prometheus        Error rate, latency, success %
  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  TOOL SELECTION:
  • ArgoCD + Argo Rollouts + Istio → Full-stack GitOps progressive delivery
  • Flux + Flagger + Linkerd → Lightweight, modular alternative
  • Istio alone → NOT enough (no GitOps state management)
  • Flagger alone → NOT enough (needs a GitOps reconciler)
  Rollouts/Flagger = controller for progressive logic. Istio = traffic plumbing.
🔬 Real-World Selection Guide — Which Combo for Which Team
Team Profile → Recommended Stack
  TEAM PROFILE              RECOMMENDED STACK           WHY
  ───────────────────────── ─────────────────────────  ────────────────────
  Small team, 1-3 clusters  ArgoCD + Argo Rollouts     Simpler, integrated
                             + NGINX Ingress            UI, fewer components
  Enterprise, 10+ clusters  ArgoCD + Argo Rollouts     Hub-spoke, SSO,
                             + Istio (hub-spoke)        centralized control
  Flux shop, modular pref   Flux + Flagger + Linkerd   Composable, per-
                                                        cluster independence
  Multi-cloud (EKS+AKS+GKE) Flux + Flagger + Istio     Multi-source (Git,
                                                        OCI, S3), flexible
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  For anihpj (small team, kubeadm): ArgoCD + Argo Rollouts + NGINX.
  Add Istio when traffic splitting needs grow beyond basic canary.

For anihpj/jobpost: Start with ArgoCD + Argo Rollouts — simplest setup, one Helm chart, integrated UI. NGINX Ingress handles basic canary via `nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/canary: "true"` annotations. If you later need A/B testing (header-based routing), add Istio. Don't over-engineer early — progressive delivery tools grow with your needs.

4.3 Pull vs. Event-Driven ReconciliationQ153–Q162

GitOps Patterns Q153 of 200
What is the difference between Pull-based and Event-driven reconciliation?
A) Pull-based: The reconciler periodically POLLS Git for changes (ArgoCD default: every 3 minutes). Event-driven: Git sends a WEBHOOK to the reconciler when a change occurs, triggering an immediate reconciliation. Both are still "Pull" in the GitOps sense (reconciler always pulls from Git) — the difference is the TRIGGER timing, not the direction of data flow. Webhooks just say "wake up and pull NOW"
B) Event-driven means Git pushes manifests directly to the cluster — no reconciler needed
C) Pull-based is deprecated — GitOps requires webhooks to be considered true GitOps
D) There is no difference — both are the same mechanism with different names
✅ Correct Answer: A

Even with webhooks, the reconciler PULLS from Git. Webhooks don't push config to the reconciler — they just notify. The reconciler always reads from Git itself. This is a key exam distinction.

📝 Detailed Explanation — Pull vs Event-Driven Architecture
Pull (Poll) vs Event-Driven (Webhook) — Both Still Pull!
  PULL-BASED (Polling):                      EVENT-DRIVEN (Webhook):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ Every 3 min:               │            │ Git push → webhook event    │
  │   Reconciler: "Changes?"   │            │   ↓                        │
  │   Git: "Yes, new commit"   │            │ Reconciler: "WOKE UP!       │
  │   Reconciler: "I'll sync"  │            │   Let me pull from Git..."  │
  │                            │            │   ↓                        │
  │ Delay: up to 3 min         │            │ Reconciler pulls from Git   │
  │                            │            │   ↓                        │
  │                            │            │ Syncs to cluster            │
  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘
  BOTH = Reconciler PULLS from Git. Difference = WHAT wakes it up.
  Timer (poll) vs Webhook (event). Reconciler ALWAYS pulls from Git.
🔬 Reconciler Internals — What Happens When "Wake Up" Fires
ArgoCD Reconciliation — Poll vs Webhook Step-by-Step
  POLL TRIGGER (every 3 min):                WEBHOOK TRIGGER (instant):
  ┌────────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. Internal timer fires    │            │ 1. GitHub sends POST to     │
  │ 2. App Controller:         │            │    /api/webhook             │
  │    List all Applications   │            │ 2. API Server validates     │
  │ 3. For each App:           │            │    HMAC signature           │
  │    a. Repo Server: clone   │            │ 3. API Server notifies      │
  │    b. Render (Helm/Kust)   │            │    App Controller           │
  │    c. Diff (3-way)         │            │ 4. App Controller:          │
  │    d. Apply if needed      │            │    "Force refresh App X!"   │
  │    e. Update status        │            │ 5. Steps b-e same as poll   │
  └────────────────────────────┘            └────────────────────────────┘
  SAME reconciliation logic. DIFFERENT trigger source.
  Webhook = instant notification. Poll = periodic health check.
  For anihpj prod: webhook ensures < 5 sec deploy. Poll catches webhook failures.

For anihpj/jobpost: Configure BOTH webhooks AND polling. Webhook for fast deploys (< 5 sec). Polling (60s interval) as fallback — if the webhook endpoint is unreachable during a network blip, the next poll cycle catches the missed commit. Redundancy = reliability.

GitOps Patterns Q154 of 200
